@@3504283 <h> Disclosure statement <p> Hugh Possingham receives funding from The Australian Research Council and the Department of the Environment and odd small grants here and there . He is affiliated with/a member of/helps out : The Wentworth Group of Concerned Scientists , Australian Wildlife Conservancy , Bush Heritage Australia , The Myer Foundation , The Thomas Foundation , WWF Australia , BirdLife Australia , the Friends of Oxley Creek Common , the Friends of Sherwood Arboretum ( patron ) , the Australian Academy of Sciences , The Nature Conservation Society of SA , Birds Queensland and a couple I will have forgotten . He is not a member of any political party . He is does occasional consulting tasks for NSW and QLD state government . He is 20% employed at Imperial College London . <p> Ramona Maggini contributed to this project while working at the ARC Centre of Excellence for Environmental Decisions and the NERP Environmental Decisions Hub at University of Queensland . <p> Andrew Reeson , Belinda Walters , Iadine Chad ? s , Jean-Baptiste Pichancourt , Jennifer Firn , Josie Carwardine , Rocio Ponce-Reyes , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , consult , own shares in or receive funding from any company or organization that would benefit from this article , and has disclosed no relevant affiliations beyond the academic appointment above . <p> Australia 's Lake Eyre is perhaps best known as the continent 's largest lake , and for the rare floods that bring the desert to life . <p> But Lake Eyre is much more than a lake . Taking into account the rivers that drain into it and where they come from , the Lake Eyre Basin is one of largest inland draining systems in the world , the size of Germany , France and Italy combined . It is home to many natural wonders , such as Uluru , and many species of threatened wildlife . <p> It is also threatened by invasive animals and plants , and climate change . How can we best protect the basin , given finite funds ? <p> In two studies ( published this week in Global Change Biology and the Journal of Applied Ecology ) and in two CSIRO reports we show that managing feral pigs is one of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in the future . <h> Introducing Lake Eyre ( Kati Thanda ) <p> Ecosystems in the Lake Eyre Basin are intimately connected when rainfall is high and water is flowing through three major river systems . Decisions within the four states that manage the basin - Northern Territory , Queensland , South Australia and New South Wales - will impact neighbouring habitats . <p> Australia 's Lake Eyre Basin covers 120 million hectares . This huge area of the outback has a rich and thriving Indigenous culture stretching back tens of thousands of years , with Indigenous people making up 40-90% of the population . <p> Invasive animals can reproduce and spread quickly , as they are highly adaptable to changing weather and biotic conditions . This combined pressure from climate change and invasive animals will impact on threatened native species already disadvantaged by habitat and environmental conditions . <p> We estimated that 29 species ( including the greater bilby ) would be at risk of extinction thanks to climate change , unless we manage other threats . <h> Getting our priorities right <p> Letter winged kiteAngus Emmott , Author provided @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is expensive , so smart decisions are necessary to make sure resources are used as efficiently as possible . <p> So we want to know managing which invasive species will give us the most bang for our buck . And we want to know that it will continue to pay off under a changing climate . <p> We combined local knowledge , scientific data and analyses to develop an efficient and rational set of strategies for managing the negative impacts of invasive species -- the priority threat management approach . <p> After comparing 11 different strategies for managing invasive species ( including dogs , cats , camels and rabbits ) we found managing feral pigs proved the most cost-effective strategy overall . <p> Pigs impact many different native species ( including flora and fauna ) . Experts estimated that pig control would have the highest uptake , a moderate cost and one of the highest benefits for threatened species . <p> Managing feral pigs would make the most difference at the least cost to the Lake Eyre Basin.Angus Emmott , Author provided <p> The benefit for threatened native wildlife of controlling @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ was considered . <p> While managing pigs overall was the best strategy , if we focus on threatened mammals we find , perhaps unsurprisingly , that managing feral predators such as cats , dogs , and foxes is the best option . <p> Invasive animals also impact on agriculture so managing them for biodiversity increases agricultural productivity . We found that managing predators ( cats , dogs and foxes ) , goats and rabbits , would potentially increase agricultural productivity by 10% or more . <p> We also found we would need to reduce invasive plants by 30% to reduce their impact , particularly parkinsonia , chinee apple and mesquite . <p> We have limited funds for protecting Australia 's environment . Prioritising what action we take can help preserve our unique biodiversity now and into the future . 
@@3504359 <p> Five months since Vanuatu was devastated by Tropical Cyclone Pam , accusations of corruption and incompetence plague reconstruction efforts . But as Antony Funnell reports from Port Vila , the tiny Pacific island nation may have even bigger problems to face . <p> Our first two attempts to descend were made through cloud so dense it blanketed the entire southwest of the island . <p> I knew the second had failed the moment the engines roared and the plane began to climb at what seemed an almost impossible angle . It was so unnerving the Filipina woman directly in front of me let out a gasp and began to pray . Efate , Vanuatu 's main island , lay somewhere below , but it might as well have been on the other side of the world . <p> As the plane levelled out and began to bank right , the captain of the Virgin Australia flight informed his passengers that the winds around the airport were so strong , and visibility so poor , that it was pointless and unsafe to try for a third time . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> The call for tourists to return is understandable and Cyclone Pam is a reminder of the acute vulnerability of tourism-dependent economies . <p> Dr Joseph Cheer , The Conversation <p> A similar turn-back had occurred only a few weeks before , according to one of the flight attendants . No need to worry ; it 's just what happens . <p> The next afternoon , on the third attempt , we finally touched down . <p> Vanuatu is known for its wild weather as much as its natural beauty . Over the past few years alone the archipelago has been buffeted by two huge cyclones -- Ivy in 2013 and Pam in early March this year . Pam was enormous -- a category five . It stripped whole regions of vegetation and left tens of thousands homeless as its 320 kilometres an hour winds tore through the country 's 83 islands . <p> Miraculously , given its savagery , only around 16 people are thought to have lost their lives . Not every island was badly affected , but the pain was widespread . <p> The first small tell-tale @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the airport . One of the signs above the International terminal is missing an L , while across the road another building marked ' VIP Lounge ' has a bright new corrugated metal roof , though a sizable chunk of its wall is still missing , the hole covered over with makeshift plastic sheeting . <p> On the way into the centre of Port Vila there are other , more dramatic signs : a hardware store bereft of its roof ; palm trees shorn of their fronds ; and the wrecks of several tourist boats smashed against the harbour 's edge , half submerged and abandoned . <p> In the main market place it 's impossible to buy bananas -- all the trees were blown away , I 'm told -- and many of the trestle tables are filled with imported products : apples and oranges sell for more than a dollar each ; expensive even by Australian standards . <p> In June , Sune Gudnitz , head of the Pacific arm of the United Nations Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs told Radio Australia that the recovery effort was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , which coordinated relief efforts across the archipelago , estimated more than 211,000 people were provided with food aid during the initial phase of recovery , with supplies flown or shipped to more than 80 islands . That initial humanitarian effort only officially ended on July 31 . <p> Much has been reconstructed in the five months since Pam , but there is clearly more to do . Australian government estimates put the economic impact of the cyclone at roughly 64 per cent of Vanuatu 's GDP . The size of the problem is far more than the tiny Pacific nation can cope with alone . <p> Last week in Port Vila , the Vanuatu government held a one-day conference for those involved in the ongoing reconstruction effort , including foreign ' development partners ' . Prime Minister Sato Kilman used the event to release a two-year strategy called the National Development Plan After Cyclone Pam . <p> A Recovery Program Committee has also been established to streamline development submissions and to ensure their implementation . <p> Kilman 's government estimates the total cost of recovery at around $674 million , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to meet . International assistance has been forthcoming , he told those present , but only around $202 million has so far been pledged or made available . <p> For its part , the Australian government has committed more than $50 million in assistance -- the first $15 million coming soon after the cyclone 's impact . In announcing the second tranche in late May , Australian Foreign Minister Julie Bishop said the money was designed to assist the government of Vanuatu with long-term recovery efforts . <p> ' This money will go a long way to help rebuild some of that critical infrastructure : schools , roads , help get people 's lives and businesses back on track to allow them to plant new crops , and also to help support really important services like health facilities for people , ' she said . <p> Bishop also made clear that any assistance from Canberra would be done in consultation with the government in Port Vila . <p> Then , in early July , Governor General Sir Peter Cosgrove visited the island of Tanna , which bore the full brunt of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Australia 's commitment and support . <p> ' What I see is a government and population focused on not giving up and not sitting back , ' he said . ' Still , there is plenty of work to do . ' <p> Cosgrove 's comments were well received in Port Vila , but the cyclone recovery and development effort has hardly been a model of cooperation and goodwill . While Prime Minister Kilman has made a point of welcoming foreign assistance , he 's also made statements that betray a disconnect between his administration and the foreign organisations and governments that want to help . <p> At last week 's National Development Plan launch , Kilman spoke of concerns about the number of foreign aid and development workers entering Vanuatu and reminded foreign development partners that it was his government 's responsibility to direct the pace of the recovery effort and its priorities . He also spoke of ensuring Vanuatu 's culture was not damaged as a result of the reconstruction effort . <p> Such sensitivity can be explained , in large part , by the criticism Kilman 's administration @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ potential impropriety . Accusations that the government has been too slow in responding to the devastation wrought by Cyclone Pam have been levelled almost from day one ; so too suggestions that a keen eye should be kept on materials and provisions earmarked for emergency response and reconstruction . <p> In early April , less than a month after the cyclone struck , the chairman of Transparency Vanuatu , the local chapter of Transparency International , declared his unease about the handling of relief supplies by local officials . While giving qualified support to the country 's National Disaster Management Office , Dr Willie Tokon nevertheless said his organisation would be carefully monitoring the NDMO 's work to ensure that aid went to the people for whom it was intended . <p> Prime Minister Kilman has since accused Dr Tokon and his organisation of mischief-making : ' Transparency International must not advocate political instability in a country that is recovering from a natural disaster , ' Kilman was quoted as saying . <p> But such concerns of potential impropriety are hardly new for the people of Vanuatu . Like many Pacific @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with graft and mismanagement . <p> In 2010 , Transparency International ranked Vanuatu at 73 on its international corruption index , only just ahead of China . Where it sits now is unclear . The organisation says a lack of available government data in recent years has prevented it from providing an accurate ranking . <p> Suggestions of corruption have dominated the political scene in Vanuatu for the entire recovery period . Indeed , such allegations have been a feature of the nation 's dysfunctional politics for years . <p> In 2004 , then prime minister Ham Lini made a public commitment to increase government transparency and institute anti-corruption measures after Australian Foreign Minister Alexander Downer threatened to cut aid to Vanuatu , which was valued at around $31 million . <p> ' Australia and Vanuatu have had our differences in recent months , ' said Mr Downer after the announcement . ' We are very happy to put them behind us . This agreement gives us confidence to continue our aid program . ' <p> But less than eight years later , corruption was once again centre-stage in Port Vila @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , used a television appearance to accuse his own civil service of misusing government funds . <p> ' Public servants in senior positions are corrupt and misuse their positions for personal gains , ' he declared , before vowing to put an end to such behaviour . <p> Kilman later lost the prime ministership , but regained it earlier this year after ousting Joe Natuman , another leader who had pledged to deal with political graft . But immediately after Kilman 's victory in early June , he found himself fending off a no-confidence motion brought by another former PM , Edward Natapei . <p> ' The recent change in government , ' said Natapei , ' will delay the implementation of Pam 's recovery programs which will affect donor partners confidence in assisting Vanuatu in the recovery projects . ' <p> The motion was unsuccessful , but it helped reinforce a general picture that Vanuatu 's parliament has become a toxic political environment . And there are good reasons why such perceptions are likely to persist . Allegations of corrupt behaviour , including bribery , have been made against 19 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ them yet another former PM , Moana Carcasses . <p> Set against such a backdrop and with the reconstruction effort only five months old , it 's little wonder the Vanuatu Daily Post recently called on the nation 's leaders to put aside their rancour and to act in ' the national interest ' . <p> ' Development in Vanuatu will only spiral further backwards if the current political game is played . The last thing it needs is political instability , ' the paper editorialised . ' This nation showed maturity in dealing with a category five cyclone . Yet it seems most of our politicians still lack maturity in leading this country . ' <p> But even as the reconstruction effort enters its next phase under the new National Development Plan After Cyclone Pam , there are still important outstanding questions to be addressed about the structure of the country 's economy and its reliance on tourism . <p> The Australian National University 's Development Policy Centre estimates that tourism makes up more than 65 percent of Vanuatu 's gross domestic product . And despite efforts since the cyclone @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to holiday , the sector has lost significant tourist dollars and is struggling to get back on its feet . Many tourist resorts , hotels and holiday apartments have reopened since the disaster , but many more are still undergoing repair . <p> In the middle of Vila Bay , directly opposite the Port Vila markets , and just a short boat ride from the town centre , sits the resort island of Iririki . The island 's many bungalows have a quintessential pacific feel : thatched roofs and criss-cross colonial verandahs that tease the water 's edge . <p> Looking across the harbour from the market , everything on Iririki seems normal . But on closer inspection , it becomes clear that the only people moving on the island are either workmen or staff . And because there are no guests , there are no lights . So as night falls the island simply vanishes into darkness . <p> While forward bookings are currently being taken , repairs on Iririki are n't expected to be finished until the end of November . Other nearby tourist facilities are ' closed until @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ similar story on other islands . <p> Worse still , the natural tropical environment that underpins Vanuatu 's tourist appeal has been significantly damaged . Travelling eastward around Efate , large sections of coastal vegetation are in tatters . What was once a thick coastal fringe of pandanus and other exotic foliage has been rendered a broken tangle . <p> A few hours out of Port Vila we stop at the popular tourist destination known as Secret Beach . The name is now a misnomer -- so much vegetation has been destroyed that the beach lies open and denuded , a hideaway no more . Given time the foliage will return , of course , but some have begun to question whether Vanuatu 's approach to tourism should remain the same . <p> Writing recently in The Conversation , Monash University tourism studies specialist Dr Joseph Cheer suggested the time was ripe for Vanuatu to use the opportunities afforded by the recovery effort to refocus its economy . <p> ' The question " if not tourism , what then ? " is inevitable , ' wrote Cheer . ' This suggests @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Such an approach is complacent economic policy that ignores the risk of relying too heavily on tourism . <p> ' The call for tourists to return is understandable and Cyclone Pam is a reminder of the acute vulnerability of tourism-dependent economies . This is not to say that tourism should be wound back . Instead , the call is for policymakers and key aid donors like Australia and New Zealand to urgently consider how Vanuatu can move beyond a disproportionate dependence on tourism . ' <p> According to Cheer , one obvious area for increased future development is agriculture . <p> ' The World Bank estimates that agriculture comprised 28 per cent of GDP in 2013 , although much is absorbed in the small domestic market and in subsistence consumption . Vanuatu 's capacity for beef , kava , tropical fruit , coffee , cocoa and other potentially high value produce must be developed . ' <p> But others , including researchers Matthew Dornan and Tess Newton Cain from the ANU 's Development Policy Centre , take a different tack . However , this growth has not benefitted ni-Vanuatu equally , with development centred in Vanuatu 's most populous island , Efate . <p> ' Few tourists venture outside of Port Vila , and few infrastructure projects are focused on development of rural areas , ' they added . ' <p> But with Vanuatu 's political system in apparent chaos , whether the nation has the leadership to adapt remains an open question . <p> What seems clear , however , is that regardless of the economic direction Vanuatu @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ likely to remain a central focus of the ' national discussion ' around development , a discussion which Dornan and Cain say began prior to Cyclone Pam , but has accelerated since the disaster . <p> Launching the first phase of construction work on a new wharf development in Port Vila on August 4 , Prime Minister Kilman once again found himself having to defend his government 's ability to act responsibly and openly . <p> ' I would like to assure our major development partners , Japan and Australia , that my government is committed to the development initiative , ' he said , ' and I am monitoring its implementation closely to ensure that all construction works are going to be implemented and completed within the promised timeframe . ' <p> Cain and Dornan talk about the perception that Vanuatu is now at a ' crossroads ' , but perhaps the country 's greatest challenge in future decades will come not just from government mismanagement or a poorly balanced economy , but also from the environment . <p> For four years running , the United Nation 's annual World @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ dangerous place to live . The rating is based on the risk of natural disaster combined with ' high social vulnerability ' . According to the report , 64 per cent of Vanuatu 's residents are exposed to natural hazards every year . <p> As I head toward Bauerfield Airport for my return flight to Australia , that vulnerability is on full show . @@3507203 <h> Discovering the genesis of our world : fragment by fragment <p> The south east coast of New South Wales contains a rich source of fish and plant fossils from the time when life moved from water to land and when forests first began to grow . A team of international scientists are studying the stories these fossils tell of a time of massive climate change and extinctions , and the beginning of the world as we know it . <p> " We 've actually got here in this part of south eastern Australia a record of rocks and fossils that represents the largest land mass from about 350 to 450 million years ago called the Gondwana continent , " says the field trip leader Dr Gavin Young from the Australian National University . <p> " Many of the things here ... only occur in this part of the world . " <p> We are standing on a very high grassy ridge looking down to the coast at a site where in the mid-70s Dr Young discovered a fossil fish . <p> " Most of the rocks around @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> In what is known as the Devonian period much of the region was covered by freshwater lakes where black shales were formed . <p> " If you went back 500 million years humans would find themselves in an alien landscape but at the time these rocks here were being laid down with their plant fossils and their fish fossils we would have felt at home because for the very first time you had land vegetation . <p> The appearance of vegetation for the first time caused carbon dioxide levels in the atmosphere to reduce and oxygen levels to increase . <p> " And everything is moving landwards from the sea , " said Dr Young . <p> " It was a major transformation in the history of life on Earth that actually led to animals living on the land . " <h> Finding a 380 million year old fish - like opening a book <p> Closer to the coast is a seam of black shale that was once well under water . <p> There 's a burst of activity when one of the scientists splits open a piece of black @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ says , " It 's like splitting open a book , and we see it for the first time in 380 million years . " <p> The team moves on to a quarry set back in the bush where road building equipment has exposed a huge number of fossil bearing rocks . <p> Hundreds of millions of years ago this was at the bottom of a lake . <p> Professor Per Ahlberg from Uppsala University in Sweden is among the team that includes scientists from France , Canada , Belgium , Germany , Britain , Spain , China , USA , and South Africa . <p> " This is following on from a conference that we had in Melbourne last week , so this is really a major international gathering . <p> " Australia has some of the finest fossils in the world from this particular time interval . <p> " We 're dealing with a time interval here when life was emerging out of the oceans onto the land . " <p> In earlier times he said that vegetation would be limited to very small plants such as mosses and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ edges , but that things then began to change very rapidly . <p> " By the time we 're looking at now , you 've got a quite complex vegetation on land probably spreading fairly far from the water courses , with the big plants being trees that are maybe 15 to 20 metres in height , with a shrubby understory and then stuff growing on the ground . <p> " But the big difference compared to today would be that major groups like flowering plants would be entirely absent . You 've got relatives of ferns and horsetails , and spore bearing plants that are now entirely extinct , and the diversity is kind of low . <p> " Trees that do n't look like anything you 'd see today but like giant versions of tree ferns - but not quite like the ones you 'd see in the forest here . <p> " What you have to imagine is this , if you are walking through one of these environments you 're not going to hear any birdsong , you wo n't even have any insects flying around , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will have is a sort of rustle in the leaf litter on the ground of scorpions , early millipedes , centipedes , things like that . " <p> He is kneeling over a large rock that has been split by earthmoving equipment exposing a flat surface of what he describes as a scattering like ' rather large bits of tea leaf ' comprising hundreds of fragments of broken down plants . <p> There is also a larger piece ' covered in a scale-like , regular diagonal pattern ' which he says is a lycopod or club moss . <h> Finding a tree from the world 's first forests <p> Then nearby two large rocks are discovered containing part of the trunk of an Archaeopteris tree . <p> Dr Robert Gess from the Albany Museum in South Africa explains it is evidence from the first big forests to grow on Earth , which were Archaeopteris forests . <p> " They entirely changed the atmospheric composition at the time . It removed so much CO2 from the atmosphere that we are creatures that have adapted to live in a world with forests . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are cutting down the forests and they 're burning all the carbon that got buried ... which goes up as greenhouse gasses . <p> " So whilst the end of the Devonian faced an extinction based on the eruption of forests and that led to global cooling , today we are running the risk of another global extinction event from quite the opposite : the destruction of forests and the release of all the carbon that was buried 300 to 360 million years ago . " <p> Dr Gess says that the importance of palaeontology is the understanding of the biosphere in terms of time . <p> " There 's a sort of illusion that we have , because we live such a short lifespan , that the world does n't change and no matter what you do to it it 's always the same . <p> Instead , he says , palaeontology teaches that life exists in a fine balance of temperature and atmospheric gas ' and that any change in those , to change just a few things , can lead to unexpectedly vast consequences ' . <p> " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ unravelling bit by bit , fragment by fragment . " <p> After around 380 million years in what once a lake bed on the far south coast of NSW a scientist split a piece of shale to reveal this tail half of an Acanthodian fish . The region is rich in fish and plant fossils from the time when life was moving from the oceans to the land . ( Bill Brown - ABC South East NSW ) 
@@3507238 <p> Western Australian researchers say they have discovered the best way to predict the impact a heatwave will have on the health system , through the same formula used to predict the heatwave itself . <p> The group from Curtin University and the health department found the formula , developed by the Bureau of Meteorology to predict the severity of a forthcoming heatwave , could also be used to accurately predict the resulting demand on health services . <p> " They BOM are using it from a meteorological point of view , looking at the climate data , and this is really about validating that , meaning that it does actually work to predict the impact on people 's health , " author Ben Scalley said . <p> The formula produces an " excess heat factor " score , resulting from contrasts between heatwave temperatures during a three-day period and the temperatures in the preceding month . <p> Dr Scalley said it would help determine when contingency plans for heatwaves should be launched . <p> " When determining or deciding when we 're going to trigger a plan @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is at each of those different points , so this study tells us clearly what the impact is at all those different trigger points so we know when best to activate our plans . " <p> Dr Scalley said the frequency , duration , and intensity of heatwaves were expected to increase over the coming years due to climate change . <p> That is predicted to double the number of heatwave-related deaths over the next 40 years , making the research vital . <p> " There are lots of things we can do to avoid the impact of heatwaves , and I think from this study and other similar studies , we 'll be moving more and more towards preventing the impact , " Dr Scalley said . <p> Heatwaves have caused more deaths in Australia in the past 200 years than any other natural hazard .

Arguably , Australian authorities have so far failed to suitably respond to the increasingly widespread use of drones ( or what they call ' Remotely Piloted Aircraft ' or RPAs ) and the concerns that arise out of their newfound popularity . As it stands , there remains in place a very strict set of rules around the use of these aircraft , especially in a commercial context . <p> The Civil Aviation Safety Authority ( CASA ) is aware of this issue and is conducting a necessary review of the Civil Aviation Safety Regulations . <p> To avoid getting into a tailspin over the rules and regulations , agencies that are thinking @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , should be aware of the following key areas of potential liability . <p> The Civil Aviation Safety Regulations ( CASR ) <p> The CASR are a complex set of rules governing all aspects of civil aviation . For advertising agencies , the first step to avoid a potentially costly nosedive is to ensure that you have a licensed drone pilot to actually fly your aircraft . <p> Agencies should also ensure that they have appropriate checks and balances in their contract with the drone operator , including warranties that the operator complies with all applicable aviation regulations . <p> Before take-off , we also suggest that agencies ask themselves the following questions : <p> Will we be operating the drone near other people ? <p> Will we be operating over roads , footpaths or other areas where there might be people ? <p> How high will we be flying ? <p> What time of day will it be ? <p> Do we need a special CASA approval for this flight ? <p> If you 're not sure about any of these questions , ask your drone operator . If you @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ legal advice to help avoid a crash landing ! <p> Negligence <p> Like any risky activities , agencies should also be aware of potential liability under the common law of negligence . If it is found that the drone operator has a duty of care to another party ( such as an onlooker ) , and the operator acts in breach of that duty , they may soon be facing a negligence action . This is particularly important for agencies who might be directing an employee or drone operator to fly the drone in a particular manner , as liability may , in some circumstances , also attach to the agency . Of course , public liability insurance is a must when operating drones for advertising purposes . <p> Privacy <p> Privacy and drones is another a hot-button issue , especially when the drone has a camera attached . A federal government committee has even held a roundtable discussion on the topic . <p> There exists a patchwork or privacy laws in Australia , but there are obvious gaps in the system in the context of drones . Existing laws include @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and territory surveillance devices laws , which make it illegal in certain circumstances to do things like record or monitor private activities . In addition , agencies should be aware of common law torts , such as trespass and nuisance , which may also apply in certain circumstances . <p> As a general rule , we believe that agencies and brands should carefully consider their position if they believe that their use of drones may constitute an invasion of privacy . In particular , we suggest making sure that you have permission if you are flying from private property and considering the need for written releases if you are using drones for the purpose of filming people . <h> Comments <p> That 's a good concise round up of where we are at with regards to drones at the moment . @@3510364 <h> Maintaining the rage beyond the golden rhythm of youth <p> 7 CommentsEllena Savage 13 August 2015 <p> In the famous essay by Joan Didion , ' Goodbye To All That ' , the author describes New York City as ' a city only for the very young ' . I first read that essay when I was very young , and like Didion leaving New York City , ' I am not that young anymore ' . <p> Didion 's New York stands in for youth , the spaces of extreme meaning and promise that become milder and less beguiling with the years . I am learning the hard way , the tried and tested way , of growing older , that I ca n't spread myself so thinly over everything I once cared about . That there are limits , and protecting them is greatly valuable . <p> However these limits do not include abandoning some idealistic sense of responsibility to the collective , however specialised that sense has become . One of the clich ? s about aging in this way , ' growing up @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about political matters . For example , ' If you 're not a liberal when you 're 20 , you have no heart . If you 're not a conservative when you 're 40 , you have no head . ' <p> The wisdom is that presumably as people age they become more reasonable . But that 's not it . As people age , if they follow a certain approved life trajectory ( acquire adequate education , attain appropriate partner ) , their lives become much easier to manage , because they establish tangible social privileges . <p> If you are not born a particular kind of affluent , youth brings the greatest financial and emotional precarity , violence , and danger . For those looking to political change during this time , the stakes are very high , and very real . <p> I suspect that this is why many radical discourses are led by young people , people under the age of 25 , and that these discourses are often so charged with extremely emotional demands . I am speaking about rage in politics , its value , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , it is one of my main motivations . It 's changed , a bit , in me , but I certainly have n't lost it . But rage is not uncomplicated . <p> In 1976 , Jo Freeman wrote an essay in Ms Magazine called ' Trashing : The Dark Side of Sisterhood ' , about her experience of being ' trashed ' , which means being excised from a political community via bullying . <p> ' Trashing is a particularly vicious form of character assassination which amounts to psychological rape , ' she writes . ' It is occasionally disguised by the rhetoric of honest conflict , or covered up by denying that any disapproval exists at all . But it is not done to expose disagreements or resolve differences . It is done to disparage and destroy . ' <p> Freeman had not wanted to write the essay , fearing it would discredit the feminist movement . But what she named became instantly recognisable as a form of social behaviour that only really occurs in youth , regardless of political associations . <p> Since writing the essay , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' If you look at statistics put out by the criminal justice system ... violent crimes are heavily concentrated among men between the ages of 15 and 25 . There 's a phrase in the system : aging out . When they get older , they commit fewer violent crimes . <p> ' I now think the reason I observed so much trashing in the younger , twentysomething branch of the movement was precisely because they were twentysomethings . They had not yet ' aged out ' of conflict . ' <p> Rawness carries the inherent risk of alienating others , because people want to protect their limits . Sometimes for good reason , other times not . <p> Everyone who speaks through or about rage knows this , knows its risks . Some argue that rage in itself has value : ' Rage is an emotion , one that comes about from feeling oppressed , ' writes Kat Muscat . Others seek to be heard through more dominant , more bourgeois , ways of speaking . I do both , consciously and unconsciously . I do n't know what to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reasons I am thinking about this so much now is that a friend and colleague recently died at 25 . She was heavily involved in our political and literary communities , and wrote and spoke provocatively about this stuff . <p> She was a radical , and drinking with her , talking with her , reading and learning from her , I saw the very best of rage and rawness in politics . She used it as a mobilising resource . But I also saw how being so close to danger , and possessing such a sense of obligation to raw , honest , and emotionally-engaged political exploration , is exhausting . Of course it is . <p> I would like to think that , for this friend , things would have gotten less exhausting as she ' aged out ' . Not that she would have lost her rage , but that it would have grown into something that would give more power than it takes . In Didion 's words , the golden rhythm of youth is , at some point , broken . What she does n't write @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ side . <p> Ellena Savage is the Editor at The Lifted Brow , commissioning Editor at Spook Magazine , and a graduate student in creative writing . <h> Comments <p> Comments should be short , respectful and on topic . @@3513324 <h> What the expansion of the Suez Canal shows about shifts in global shipping <h> Author <h> Disclosure statement <p> Satya Savitzky does not work for , consult , own shares in or receive funding from any company or organization that would benefit from this article , and has disclosed no relevant affiliations beyond the academic appointment above . <p> Egypt has opened a second lane to the Suez Canal amid much fanfare . The US$8 billion dollar expansion adds 35km of new channels to the existing canal and another 35km where existing bodies of water were dredged to make way for larger ships . This will supposedly increase capacity from 50 transits a day to 97 and cut waiting times from 18 to 11 hours , which the Suez Canal Authority claims will more than double annual revenue to US$13.2 billion by 2023 . <p> By cutting the distance between Europe and Asia by 43% , the Suez Canal 's opening in 1869 dramatically lowered the cost of moving goods between the two continents . Suez -- like the Panama Canal , which is also currently undergoing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of national economies , and accelerated globalisation . <p> Yet in the 21st century , driven largely by growth in China 's manufacturing exports , there are now many more ships plying the sea lanes . And these ships are increasing in size . The world 's largest is the size of four football fields and can carry 19,000 20-foot cargo containers . As a result , the narrow passages that provide economical links between centres of production and consumption -- like Panama , Suez and Malacca -- are as much obstacles to global trade as facilitators of it . <p> Imperatives to smooth the flow of commodities , as well as provide insulation from the disruptions of piracy , are also driving the development of alternative routes . These include the new Chinese-backed Silk Road initiative that connects China to Europe over land and sea , and the Russian-controlled Northern Sea Route through the Arctic , which is becoming more accessible as a result of climate change processes . The expansion of Suez is therefore part of a much larger picture of global growth in shipping . <h> Laden with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and economics . They are culturally and symbolically laden -- particularly because they involve re-tracing historical trade routes . European legacies of discovery and natural conquest live on through the desire to master routes across the Arctic . And similarly the Chinese government 's Silk Road initiative , dubbed " one belt , one road " , invokes an imagined community of trade , linking China to both its neighbours and its past . <p> As anthropologist Brian Larkin proposes , material infrastructure is often implicated in dynamics that have little to do with their technical functions . The carving of these routes is as caught up with the carving of national identities , as it is with extending a state 's geopolitical reach and increasing its revenue . The recent opening of the new Suez Canal is as much a symbolic response to terrorist threats by the Egyptian government as it is an attempt to bolster Egypt 's global standing . <p> Massive : a CSCL Globe shipping container , the size of four football fields.Keith , CC BY-SA <p> Indeed the speed of the canal 's upgrade has astonished @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ now seem subject to the same accelerated cycles of upgrade and obsolescence as the goods that move through them . Drummed up by their architects and promoters , there is also a similar sense of hype and anticipation that is built around these projects , which invariably all promise to transform global trade . <h> Future problems <p> Yet , even with the expansions , Suez and Panama will still not be able to accommodate the world 's biggest oil tankers and container ships , making them in a sense , already obsolete . A Chinese consortium , however , is developing a new 175-mile long canal through Nicaragua , which will be capable of handling the world 's biggest vessels . The project will provide an alternative to the Panama Canal , but faces vociferous opposition from the farmers , peasants and indigenous groups whose lands are set to be annexed by this new artery of global trade . Thousands of people were reportedly evicted from their homes to facilitate Suez 's expansion too . <p> Not everyone 's happy about the growth.EPA/Mario Lopez <p> There are inevitable ecological implications @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ scale too . Suez 's opening in the late 19th century not only facilitated the global movement of goods , but the massive transfer of invasive species . Some 350 non-native species are thought to have entered the Mediterranean via the canal , many of which pose significant threats to human health and local ecosystems . <p> For instance swarms of " nomad jellyfish " , which entered the Mediterranean via Suez in the 1970s , frequently make commercial fishing impossible , can close tourist beaches , and have even temporarily disabled a power station . The enlargement of the canal , scientists have warned , will increase the number of invasions . <h> Footprints in the sea <p> Unlike paths etched through landscapes , oceans retain no footprints . But the intensity of modern-day dredging , as well as the largescale transfer of marine biota , means that seascapes will bear the mark of these projects long after the activities which gave rise to them cease . <p> The expansion of Panama , scheduled for completion next year , has triggered a wave of frenetic dredging activity along the Atlantic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ losing traffic to competitors . New York 's port alone has moved some 42m cubic yards of dirt over a decade in anticipation of the opening of an expanded Panama Canal . Thousands of years from now , archaeologists will be able to find the channels carved through our oceans by all this movement . <p> So , while the economic significance of the expanded Suez is in doubt , its symbolic value is certain . Its legacy is unlikely to be its consequences for global trade , but to further contribute to such a dramatic reshaping of the planet by human activity . 
@@3513370 <h> Radiant Pavilion goes beyond the bling in a week-long celebration of wearable art <h> Liza Power <p> Looking at jewellery can be a staid affair : exquisite objects in glass boxes that glint seductively but lack the one thing that brings them to life : a wearer . Framed by clothing , draped around necks , wrists , fingers or ears , catching the light as people move ; that 's when jewellery realises its beauty and spirit . <p> This is what curators Chloe Powell and Claire McArdle were thinking about when they imagined Radiant Pavilion , a seven-day celebration of contemporary jewellery that opens in Melbourne in September . Their program includes exhibitions , but it also spans urban interventions , performances and installations . Its focus lies not only in exploring the breadth of contemporary jewellery practice , but also the intimate , sophisticated and unexpected ways that forms of adornment underpin our sense of identity and the way we connect with people . Taking in 57 events at sites across the city , from laneways to butcher 's shops , theatres to op @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ see your favourite brooch in a very different light . <p> Powell and McArdle are members of Melbourne 's close-knit jewellery community ; craftspeople and curators who mostly confine themselves to studio work benches making world-renowned work . Radiant Pavilion is their chance to come out and play . <h> Radiant Pavilion <p> McArdle is an established name ; she has held six solo exhibitions and undertaken residencies in Australia , Mexico , Iceland and Estonia . She won first prize at the Contemporary Wearables Biennial Award in 2013 and her work has been exhibited in Thailand , Hong Kong , the US , Britain , Germany , France , Austria and the Netherlands . Powell has been working in the field since 2007 , recently co-ordinating the inaugural Mari Funaki Award for Contemporary Jewellery and before that assisting at Amsterdam 's Gerrit Rietveld Academie . At the latter she worked with Dutch curator Liesbeth den Besten on her book On Jewellery : An International Compendium of International Art Jewellery . <p> Advertisement <p> The women have been discussing Radiant Pavilion for more than a year . " <p> McArdle finds the most fascinating aspect of her craft in the intimate connection people form with the adornments they wear . She loves the stories people tell of their precious collections : where they were bought , why , if they were inherited from a family member , or their significance as good luck charms or talismans . <p> She talks of the role jewellery plays across cultures and centuries , conveying status and rank , travelling down lineages , marking shifts in innovation and technology through the use of materials and methods : handmade one-offs or machine-made mass productions . <p> Meet Me is an exhibition of Minna Loft 's jewellery that captures periods of time the artist has spent working with women of various cultures and backgrounds and exchanging skills and ideas in the process . She is interested in ideas of authenticity , the ephemeral and tangible . She describes the process as " utilising the language and formality of a garment care label and applying it to a person " , and says the work " is about returning care to the self , our relationships with friends , family and the wider community " . " It 's a tongue-in-cheek look at acknowledging our flaws and vulnerabilities , a warning , an offering . " During an event at RMIT Design Hub , she 'll sew labels into audience members ' garments ; attendees will be surprised when they collect their jackets from the cloakroom afterwards . <p> Site 3065 , by Manon van Kouswijk and Roseanne Bartley , will unfurl along the streets , lanes and shop fronts of Fitzroy in a series of performances and displays , while dis/appear , by Thai artist Taweesak Molsawat , will be performed on the steps of Flinders Street Station at 12.30pm and 5pm daily for a week . <p> Powell and McArdle hope Radiant Pavilion will engage more Melburnians in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ extends from Blacksmith Doris , a Bundoora-based group run by women to teach and learn the craft of blacksmithing , to the gallery spaces of Anna Schwartz , where Uncommon Moments , a performance by Susan Cohn , will be staged . @@3513398 <p> Happy Earth Overshoot Day , that singular day each year when humanity 's resource demands outstrip the planet 's ability to regenerate them . Last year , we faced our ecological debt on August 19th , this year , we 've reached this milestone six days earlier . Well done . <p> Overshoot Day is estimated every year by the Global Footprint Network , an international think-tank that synthesizes data on Earth 's depressingly dwindling resource supply . The calculation used to determine the fateful day is simple : <p> To determine the date of Earth Overshoot Day for each year , Global Footprint Network calculates the number of days of that year that Earth 's biocapacity suffices to provide for humanity 's Ecological Footprint . The remainder of the year corresponds to global overshoot . <p> Earth Overshoot Day is computed by dividing the planet 's biocapacity ( the amount of ecological resources Earth is able to generate that year ) , by humanity 's Ecological Footprint ( humanity 's demand for that year ) , and multiplying by 365 , the number of days in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ equation are n't always easy to come by . Earth 's ' biocapacity ' refers to biologically productive regions of the land and sea , while our ' ecological footprint ' includes global carbon emissions , resource demands for crops , and our consumption of fish stocks and forests . Some of these variables , including productive land , are in constant flux due to natural disasters and climate change . Others , like fish stocks , are sketchy because humans like to lie . <p> But regardless of the error bars , the overall direction of the trend is crystal clear . According to the GFN , humanity 's consumption of resources first started to exceed Earth 's capacity in the early 1970s . Overshoot Day has been arriving earlier every since , right in step with global population growth and resource consumption . In 2000 , we slid into ecological debt in early October , now , we 've rolled debt day back to midsummer . <p> You do n't have to be a scientist to sense that the mere existence of Overshoot Day is very bad news . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , your bank stops lending your broke arse money . Ecological debt is even more pernicious , because it depletes the very resources we depend on to survive . <h> One holiday we could do without <p> How to eliminate Overshoot Day ? Well , at our current rate of resource consumption , the GFN reckons we need about 1.6 Earths to break even -- so basically , we need Earth , a fully terraformed Mars , and maybe a Moon colony for good measure . But with roughly a billion humans still living in extreme poverty , we might need quite a bit more space if we want to support everyone comfortably . I somehow feel like there 's going to be a waiting list for the first colony around Saturn . <p> Anyway , since we 're not going to be terraforming ourselves a second breathable planet in the next 10,000 years , the only way this game ends well for us is for developed countries to cut back on their overall resource consumption , switch to renewable energy sources , and recycle more . And for the human @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ If there 's one key takeaway from Ecology 101 , it 's that exponentially multiplying species are usually short-lived . <p> Without looking it up at all , I 'm assuming this annual number was calculated by how much we can use a year at a reasonable sustainable level . So if we do n't go over our quota each year , the human race can continue to exist for however many years this number has been calculated for . I 'm guessing this fact is important , because if we constantly go over our annual amount , we 'll be in trouble in only a few decades . <p> " But the numbers we plug into that equation are n't always easy to come by . Earth 's ' biocapacity ' refers to biologically productive regions of the land and sea , while our ' ecological footprint ' includes global carbon emissions , resource demands for crops , and our consumption of fish stocks and forests . Some of these variables , including productive land , are in constant flux due to natural disasters and climate change . Others , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lie . " -- So that paragraph said , in short , the numbers are make believe . The assertion " because humans like to lie " means they can plug essentially any number into any of the fields of the equation . The data they collect is wrong or a " lie " . I call bad science on that . Furthermore , they ( Global Footprint Network ) collect the data that tells them worldwide acreage of productive land , in the same year that it is relevant . That would be extremely expensive , if you think about it- bad science . Play the devils advocate Maddie Stone , disagree with a source and have them back up their claim . @@3515229 <h> Waterstreet life : a shark 's tale of woe on the Great Barrier Reef <h> Charles Waterstreet <p> When sharks start eating our hero surfers like Mick Fanning , everyone gets up in arms . Photo : World Surf League <p> Up north , sharks are on everybody 's lips -- and vice versa . When sharks start eating our hero surfers like Mick Fanning , everyone gets up in arms -- before they lose them . From Ballina to Byron Bay , sharks are more feared than the Islamic State . Some are larger than a Toyota Hiace and need " massive balls of fish " just to keep cruising . Boardies and deckhands are demanding a cull ; there 's blood in the water and rage on dry land . Even though it 's winter , every shark 's bite could be their last swallow before summer . <p> Further north , on the Great Barrier Reef , swims Shark Girl -- aka Madison Stewart . A husky-voiced , short , dark-haired siren of the seas around the reef , Shark Girl fights to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Heritage-listed marine park surrounding the Great Barrier Grief , as it 's rapidly becoming . She comes to lunch with Dan Ackroyd and his family , who are in Australia promoting Crystal Head Vodka and visiting the GBR as part of their ecological journeying around the globe . My son has been with them on archeological digs for dinosaur bones in Canada and Alaska . <p> Dan Ackroyd 's appetite for life is only surpassed by his desire for knowledge . He 's a walkin ' , talkin ' Google . At his request , I gather Malcolm and Lucy Turnbull ( old friends from an earlier Reef trip ) and Peter Woolcott ? , the ambassador for climate change and his wife Tanya . Shark Girl stands up on her chair and controls our table like an angler playing with a trout . Stewart first wet her feet on the GBR at two years of age , before living there on a yacht with her parents . She left school at 14 to live among the sharks who inhabit the Reef . These sharks are the apex predators in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ environment . Apex predators like sharks directly limit the populations of their prey , which in turn affects the prey of those animals and so on . Conservation group OCEANA says " without apex predators , there 's a potential for unchecked predation by other lower predatory species , overeating of vegetation by herbivorous species , and increased competition that ultimately affects the species ' richness and abundance " . <p> When 78,000 sharks are caught by licensed fishermen and sold as " flake " in fish and chips joints around Australia , you might think that leaves a gap in the equilibrium of life . Amidst the ecosystem that lives so delicately on the reef , the biggest threat is not the large man-eaters , but man eating sharks . With 1600 tonnes eaten a year , what do you get ? You get an ecology thrown out of whack and the whole chain of life is disturbed so that the safety and security of the literal bottom feeder , the reef itself , is starved of the nutrients that millions of years have carefully provided . <p> Advertisement <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will never make the mistake of Labor , he will never put ecology above economy , you can now kiss the reef goodbye . For about $20 million you can compensate the fishermen and ensure 130 species of sharks and rays that inhabit that habitat continue their vital work of eating the older , sicker , slower fish down the chain and preserving nature 's trusted way . <p> The reef has been strangled by the pollution seeping out of the rivers and drains into the waters around it . This industrial , mining and farming pollution can be cured by cracking down on the transport , horticulture and drilling that sends deadly chemicals into the waters around the soft coral groves . It 's killing the reef by eating it alive . The rise in the water temperature due to global warming is rotting the reef , but it is a worldwide problem . The sharks that live and stay around the reef are the harmless loafers of the sea . Only the odd visiting Tiger Shark or Great White actually presents any possible sense of danger . The reef @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ started to decay . <p> At our table , Shark Girl stands her ground and argues for a moratorium on shark fishing , which has worked in Mexico . Shark Girl also speaks of the tiny island of Palau which was the first country in the world to declare its waters a shark sanctuary , banning the sale and illicit trade of shark fins to bourgeoisie in China . <p> The creatures are not just part of the ecology , but in a long-sighted way , essential to our economy as tourism becomes the major industry in Australia , as its minerals and coals become cheaper elsewhere . Australia is a classic tourist trap , fair and square , and the sooner we face it and preserve it , the better . <p> Shark Girl wins the hearts and minds of most at the table with her stirring defence of the reef sharks around her wetlands home . I do n't think I 'll ever eat flake again . As Spike Milligan says , there 's a world of difference between man eating shark and man-eating sharks . Let 's leave @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ nibbling each other . <p> It was a marvellous lunch , and I take a selfie with Shark Girl . Danny Ackroyd served his famous CHV in small skull glasses and I wander back to chambers to prepare for the next day . There are sharks everywhere .

After two days hearing from some of the biggest players in B2B , I now think that 's at least a possibility . <h> The QR code is back ( but not as we know it ) <p> While QR codes failed to live up to the hype in linking the physical and digital worlds there are a lot @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Tesoriero <p> No less than five years ago , traditional QR codes were promising us all huge things . Although adopted late through advertising circles , they were destined to be the big break we were all looking for : the simple connection between people and brands , allowing a quick , seamless interaction from real world to digital . Goodbye awkward thumb-typing of URLs into mobiles , hello speedy brand loyalty and integrated reward systems . <p> All amazing stuff -- but the only problem was , nobody was using them . New users got frustrated downloading clunky third-party QR code reader apps and confused about how advertisers were applying them . Today however , that hurdle may have been overcome , as a clever adaption of the traditional QR code paves the way for its next evolution . <p> Innovating a fresh approach <p> A few months back , we were brainstorming new tech ideas and began investigating Snapchat 's new functionality of scanning Snapcodes as an easier way to add new friends . The notion is simple : Friend ' A ' simply opens Snapchat on their phone @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' then opens Snapchat on their phone and takes a photo of that Snapcode -- then hey presto , friend ' A ' and ' B ' have automagically connected on Snapchat . <p> This got us thinking , " what if a brand could connect like that ? " And what if Snapcodes could be on signs , stickers , banners or billboards ? People could instantly connect with the brands , content or competitions they wanted , directly through their Snapchat network . <p> As it turns out , a small handful of early adopters had a similar train of thought . Several weeks later Call of Duty , one of the world 's most profitable video game franchises , incorporated Snapcodes on pictures and signs located within their latest game . It was a move that pulled them a quarter of a million new Snapchat followers in less than a month . In a completely different industry , we also discovered that internationally renowned DJ , Steve Aoki , had plans to project his Snapcode at concerts and print it on t-shirts -- another clever use of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ his entire fan base . <p> The rebirth of QR codes <p> It became apparent that we were seeing the revival of QR codes as a marketing tool . But what 's so different ? Well for users , firstly , it 's a more intuitive , native based experience . There 's no need to download any clunky third-party QR readers as Snapchat has the code-reading functionality embedded within the app -- integrating an intuitive behaviour which people open then use every day . When adding a new Snapchat friend ( or brand ) , the user then simply takes a photo of the Snapcode with their Snapchat camera ( another tool already used every day ) . <p> The Snapcode itself is also a simple visual to engage with . Unlike the rather chaotic looking traditional QR code , the dotted code surrounding an already trusted Snapchat logo visually spells out simple instructions : " Scan this sign and we 'll be connected through Snapchat " . <p> Pioneers leading the charge <p> In recent times , Shazam has also joined the QR code comeback party with their introduction @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ visually scan the Shazam logo on advertising and products within the real world environment for more content . Technically it 's a similar concept to Snapcodes and a great step forward , but Shazam is n't a social network , which makes a difference for ongoing personalised engagement and brand loyalty ( with good marketing , that is ) . <p> Either way , this all has the potential to be a huge win for brands . Not only is it becoming evident that consumers are re-engaging with these simplified , yet innovative adaptions of QR codes , but ( within the Snapchat example ) brands also have the ability to link directly back to the user 's personal social media account . This means instant access to priceless user information and the ability to continually connect with that person in the future . <p> Who else wants to play ? <p> But Snapchat is only one social network with a niche youth target audience ( and a female skew ) . So believe me , from personal experience , Snapchat can often be a difficult platform to sell into clients @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's Snapcode ? And a close second question -- where is Instagram , Twitter and Pinterest ? These are now highly monetised networks with the potential to connect with people in one simple action . <p> No more brands pleading for ' likes ' , or asking for sign-ups to Facebook apps , just a simple request for common user behaviour . Snap a photo and connect . Why stick a useless Facebook logo on your advertising when a Facebook " code " could instantly immerse them in the message your brand is trying to convey ? <p> There 's actually numerous ways a Facebook " code " could become useful . If they 're clever , it could even become a payment wallet system to compete with the upcoming Apple Pay ( but perhaps that 's another article in itself ) . More importantly for now though , as a creative director eternally searching for that seamless , simple link from the real world to digital ideas , the sooner all social networks take a leaf out of Snapchat 's book , the better . <h> Comments <p> George 16 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ well and doing exactly what we need them to : for example , you ca n't really bootstrap two-factor authentication for google , amazon , or a host of other places without them . @@3519236 <h> The Pinkwashing of Corporate Australia : for profit or pride ? <h> The LGBTI community needs to make sure organisations are aware that pinkwashing is used as a positive tool and done correctly . Steven Spencer explains . <p> DID you apply a rainbow filter to your profile picture to celebrate marriage equality in the US ? I 'm afraid you 're nothing but a piece of data to be analysed and used . <p> Does this make you feel a bit squeamish ? Or is this an acceptable price to pay to show your pride ? It 's not just us -- companies are draping themselves in our rainbow flag , our symbol of pride , the banner we 've used to unite us ever since we were cast aside to the fringe of society . <p> Pinkwashing is an act in which institutions co-opt LGBTI symbols and issues to present itself as gay-friendly , often as a means to distract from or disguise unethical behaviour . <p> The term has become synonymous with the Israeli government 's marketing of itself as a progressive , gay-friendly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Locally , we see the big four banks supporting LGBTI causes while also investing in fossil fuel projects and heavy-polluter projects . <p> Does one action outweigh the other ? For an example , just because your bank shows pride in our rainbow community does n't mean you should n't divest from them for investing in projects that contribute to climate change . <p> But how can a company show pride ? It 's just a soulless moneymaking machine , right ? It 's not a person ? The battlefront for marriage equality in Australia reveals much about the state of gay liberation , gay identity and correspondingly the role and responsibilities of corporations that are rapidly outgrowing their role as money-making machines and spilling over into serving a role as citizen , political player and rainbow flag-bearer . <p> On May 29 , 53 Australian corporations took out a full-page advertisement in The Australian newspaper with Australian Marriage Equality and " came out " in support for marriage equality , followed up by advertisements through other outlets . This unprecedented show of support for a highly-politicised and divisive issue @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ papers was viewed largely as a moment of solidarity for corporate Australia and the marriage equality movement . <p> However , hidden behind the positive message and progressive call-to-action , there were other messages that have great implications for corporate strategy and the LGBTI community . This was a veiled public attack on politicians and political parties that oppose marriage equality and by association , those Australians that oppose the issue . It was a populist statement used as an affordable publicity opportunity and it was an easy grab for the lucrative " pink dollar " . The influence exerted in this case shows it is probably better to have them with us rather than against us . <p> It may be seen as a progressive and political move , but really it 's a safe move to show support our community , as opposed to before when we were cast aside . It 's our popular moment and everyone wants to sit with us . <p> The introduction of marriage equality in Ireland and the US has pushed this issue to the forefront of social policy reform in Australia after @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ support amongst the broader population at around 70 per cent . Individuals , religious leaders , politicians and businesses have all come out in support of this reform . However , why wo n't corporate Australia speak on other politicised but very important issues , such as asylum seekers and climate change ? <p> We 're a comparably safe topic to talk about , as long as we do n't damage their brand marching up Oxford St or down Fitzroy St with my jockstrap on . <p> Pinkwashing is often adopted to meet internal corporate social responsibility requirements ( the diversity " tick-box " on the annual report ) , to attract human capital and to increase sales and brand image and loyalty . <p> At the end of the day , does supporting a safe and popular issue help your financial performance and is that the main contributing factor -- or is there a degree of altruism involved ? Do we see these corporations demonstrating support for that community in more material ways ? Gay pride events , LGBTI charities and non-profits have relied on support from corporate Australia in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rights with marriage should also be bankrolled . <p> Feeling co-opted yet ? Does the corporation have any role to use our rights and pride whether it benefits us or not ? As a consumer group , we 've always been able to spot a fake when we see one -- just because a local store displays a rainbow flag in their window does n't mean they foster a homo-accepting culture in the workplace , nor do we accept a large corporation sponsoring our queer events without donating to our charities . <p> We may be getting pinkwashed , but our " pink dollar " is our biggest bargaining chip . Let 's use it wisely and to support broader issues now that we 've got a seat at the table . <p> An important question we need to ask ourselves is whether or not we 're okay with this . Should we have a price ? It 's better to have them on our side than against us , right ? Corporate citizenship and power is only increasing , so riding that wave can certainly benefit us -- but the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not wish to prescribe to the new paradigm may miss the wave . <p> Stop the pinkwash or tickle me pink ? Our community has some soul-searching to do and Australian organisations need to make sure they listen . <p> Steven Spencer is an activist in the field of sexual health who has just launched a blog on sexual health and community wellbeing called Mother Superior : www.mothersuperior.info . Follow him on Twitter : @StevenStorven

@@3522119 <h> The challenge of managing Earth 's new economic frontier : our oceans <h> Author <h> Disclosure statement <p> Alistair McIlgorm works for the Australian National Centre for Ocean Resources and Security ( ANCORS ) at the University of Wollongong . He has been Director of Dominion Consulting Pty Ltd since 1997 . He has recently received funding from the Fisheries Research and Development Corporation , the Department of Industry NSW ( Fisheries ) , the Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade ( DFAT ) and the David and Lucille Packard Foundation . He has been invited to speak on the Blue Economy and review studies by the Chinese Government State Oceans Administration , the Indian Government Ministry of External Affairs and Partnerships in Environmental Management for the Seas of East Asia ( PEMSEA ) , Manila , Philippines . He is on the Editorial Board of the Journal of Ocean and Coastal Economics , Monterey , US . <p> Amid growing demand for seafood , gas and other resources drawn from the world 's oceans , and growing stresses from climate change , we examine some of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " in smarter , more sustainable ways . <p> As the world 's land-based economies struggle with annual rates of around 2% growth in Gross Domestic Product , the global marine economy -- now increasingly being talked about as " the blue economy " -- is a bright light on the horizon . <p> New developments in marine industries range from diversification in aquaculture to the new technologies of marine bio-technology and pharmaceuticals , marine renewable energy such as wind and wave power , and deep sea mining . <p> The European Commission has a " blue growth " strategy , recognising that those marine industries are growing at a faster rate than those on Europe 's land mass . And in areas such as south east Asia , the oceans contribute directly to the wellbeing of hundreds of millions of people . <p> In Australia , a new national report forecasts marine industries ' contribution to Australia 's Gross Domestic Product will grow three times faster in the next decade than the rest of the economy -- doubling from A$47.2 billion a year to about A$100 billion in 2025 . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and as a significant source of food and energy , the ocean 's contribution is already important . This century , it is likely to become an economic force . <p> But those researchers also noted many challenges ahead , including " how to create predictable cash flows for investors behind the valuable ecosystem services offered by the ocean " . <p> The consensus is that going from the ocean economy to the emerging blue economy will take time . But there are still major questions about the blue economy we need to answer -- including how to define , measure and govern it , especially when so much of the world 's ocean lies beyond national borders . <p> But among the marine community and increasingly in government and global business circles , there has been a shift from talking about " the ocean economy " to " the blue economy " . <p> While there is still no broad agreement on what that means , The Economist recently adopted this working definition : <p> A sustainable ocean economy emerges when economic activity is in balance with the long-term capacity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and healthy . <p> Economic growth is not enough ; we also need to do a better job of sustainably managing ocean resources . <p> A snapshot of some of the challenges ahead for the blue economy , from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development ( OECD ) . <p> There have been several drivers behind that shift . The past decade has seen the incorporation of green accounting concerns about the sustainable development arising through the United Nations ' Rio 20+ process . <p> Small islands , such as Seychelles and Mauritius , have led the way in calling for the use of marine resources and eco-system services to be sustainable . Internationally , new approaches to environmental accounting have been developing . <p> This need for greener and more sustainable marine activities has also been influenced by climate change . In amongst approaches involving ecosystem values lurks the longstanding need to control the impacts from marine industries , such as overfishing and pollution . Controlling industry is necessary -- and to do so , having a good understanding of the economics matters . <p> Yet marine economists have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the ocean economy are not in place . <p> For a start , what are we measuring ? Whose job is it to measure it ? Who has the vision for the future of the blue economy ? How do we get the private sector to invest ? And is there a governance framework that will support private investment in ocean or " blue " industries ? <h> Opportunities and challenges for south east Asia <p> Together with economic growth and long-term environmental sustainability , social equity is another crucial consideration -- particularly in booming regions such as south east Asia , with so many coastal communities dependent on the ocean . <p> So where is the blue economy going in this region ? <p> Any country looks at what their comparative advantage may be in benefiting from the sea . For example , Malaysia 's economic strength has been shipping , due to its location beside the Strait of Malacca . <p> In China there are already an estimated 32.7 million jobs or more than 4.15% of total national employment in " ocean-related sectors " . Interestingly , The Economist @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ China 's core " major ocean industries " . <p> That 's a good illustration of why it 's so important to be clear about what we 're measuring when talking about the blue economy . In this case , the Chinese government 's higher figure factors in additional jobs in support areas ( such as the scientific , research , education and ocean management service sectors ) , ocean-related enterprises ( businesses with economic and technical links to marine industries ) , plus indirect and induced employment . <p> Seaweed is just one of China 's many marine exports . It supplies 13.5 million tonnes of seaweed a year for gels , hydrocolloids and alginates to the global food industry . <p> China has a clear comparative advantage in bio-technology , genetic improvement of seaweeds and development of marine pharmaceuticals . But its " blue economy " challenges include reducing coastal pollution so that it can maintain and increase the sustainable value of marine industries . <h> Opportunities and challenges for Australia <p> What about Australia ? Well , with production of 2,000 tonnes of seaweed a year , the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in several corners of the country ( including South Australia and Wollongong , a few seaweed experimental enterprises are developing niche opportunities . <p> However , there are much bigger industries at work along our coastline . For instance , earlier this year the Reserve Bank of Australia said that liquified natural gas was expected to become Australia 's second largest commodity export ( after iron ore ) by 2018 . It 's also been predicted that port visits by ships to Australia will double by 2030 . <p> Tourism is another good example of the opportunities -- and challenges -- that lie ahead . <p> Speaking to a Chinese delegate at a recent blue economy workshop , I mentioned a luxury resort being built by a Chinese entrepreneur on Australia 's Gold Coast , which is expected to bring in 30,000 Chinese tourists a year . <p> As a lady well across her national statistics , the delegate replied , " Actually , last year we only had 1.2 million people going abroad ... not that many when you consider our population " ( 1.3 billion ) . <p> But @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ use of our national biodiversity ? Or are we just " the lucky country " , with offshore gas for exports and beaches to attract tourists ? Can we be more than a quarry and holiday destination for the world ? <p> Like so much about the blue economy , those are questions we 're yet to clearly answer . 
@@3522291 <p> Most of us have sat through a science fiction movie , relished the plot and the suspense -- and then got home wondering what was fact or fiction . <p> Are we just spoilsports ?
