( inanimate objects contaminated with infectious organisms ) -- and , after a breakdown in social order , the eventual formulation of a vaccine to stop the virus in its tracks . <p> True , a virus that makes an inter-species jump is usually lethal to the new hosts , Dr Sussex says . " But this one not only is highly infectious but kills fast . " <p> A truly efficient pandemic virus , she points out , would have a long , slow @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as possible before symptoms alerted the public health authorities " . <p> As for the vaccine : " Ebola was identified in 1976 , and they 've now only got a vaccine that seems to work 100 per cent in trials , " Dr Sussex says . <p> " In Contagion , one living monkey does not a successful vaccine make . No sane virologist would inject themselves at this point ; there 's absolutely no guarantee it would work on humans . " <p> Science-fiction writer Andrew Macrae is more upbeat about the movie 's factual content . <p> " Hard science fiction , which emphasises scientific detail and technical accuracy , takes an idea grounded in current knowledge and by a rational and methodical process of extrapolation turns it into a speculative premise , " Dr Macrae says . <p> This is the principle underlying much of science fiction 's golden age in the 1940s and 50s , when a protagonist would use science to solve story problems . <p> " A film like Contagion , grounded as it is in real , plausible science , provides the human @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> " It allows an audience to make emotional sense of the threat of a global pandemic , and thus can help prepare us for the real thing -- or at least let us explore ideas from different perspectives . " <p> In part , she says , that 's because the movie virus , called MEV-1 , is based on the science of a real microbe -- the nipah virus -- which is studied at the CSIRO 's Animal Health Laboratory near Geelong . <p> " From the shots in the lab , the PowerPoint slides used in meetings -- even the way they talk to each other -- it 's a scientist 's sci-fi movie , " Dr Evans says . <p> " That does n't mean it is n't enjoyable for non-scientists . This is what makes Contagion quite a reasonable sci-fi story because it actually explores this dimension from quite a number of perspectives . @@3522320 <p> Melbourne might once again have been crowned the world 's most liveable city , but Toby Kent is more focused what could one day knock us off that perch . <p> Heatwaves , climate change , influenza pandemics and cyber attacks . These are the kinds of threats that Mr Kent is currently drawing up a citywide plan to ensure Melbourne is " resilient " enough to handle and maintain our quality of life . <p> Mr Kent is Melbourne 's " chief resilience officer " , a perhaps oddly titled position that has been funded by the US-based Rockefeller Foundation as part of a global program to strengthen cities . <p> In his first media interview since taking on the role at Melbourne City Council , Mr Kent told Fairfax Media that amid the potential threats facing the city 's livability was complacency . <p> Advertisement <p> " We do have a tremendous quality of life and we need to not just assume it is always going to be great , " he said . <p> The resilience of a city is effectively how prepared it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ well it responds when they hit . But that is not necessarily just a test of how well equipped our emergency services are ( though of course it is part of it ) . <p> " We have probably built a culture that is probably a little too reliant on emergency services , " Mr Kent said . <p> The plan will look at how to improve and strengthen many aspects of the city - its environment , infrastructure and the connections between government , business and communities - as a way to buffer Melbourne against problems that may loom and ensure its livability remains world class . <p> In particular , Mr Kent says a major theme will be how communities can be empowered to do more for themselves , so police , firefighting and ambulances services are used as a top up for when things go really go wrong . A big part of that is helping Melbourne 's residents make better connections to each other and ward off growing community isolation . <p> As an example Mr Kent pointed to a program run by some local councils asking @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in need , with the two connected online . If a family is prepared to look after that person in good times , then they are more likely to do the same in a crisis . <p> " What we keep coming back to is about how do we actually equip people to be better prepared to help one another , " Mr Kent said . <p> The Melbourne resilience plan is due to be finished mid-next year and is being designed with the involvement of over 30 local councils , along with emergency management , business and community input .

@@3525390 <h> Climate changing too fast to predict ' Godzilla ' drought <p> Australia is set to experience one of the strongest El Ninos ever recorded . Photo : AAP <p> A huge El Nino is predicted for this Australian summer , but one scientist says the rapid pace that climate change is making means existing weather models simply wo n't be able to predict what the future holds . <p> El Nino is the term used to describe a warming of oceans in the eastern Pacific that disrupts weather patterns and causes a corresponding cooling of the ocean in the western Pacific and around Northern Australia , resulting in extreme droughts in eastern Australia . <p> The report comes on the back of predictions that Australia is this summer set to experience one of the strongest El Ninos ever recorded , ordinarily meaning an extremely hot and dry summer for south eastern Australia and the late arrival of monsoon season in the north . <p> However , Australian National University ( ANU ) climate scientist Bradley Opdyke says the Nature Climate Change study was not based on models @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are drawing a long bow to come to the conclusions they have , to be honest , " he said . <p> Experts say extreme wet weather is on the way . Photo : Shutterstock <p> " You ca n't use the previous data anymore , as the baseline is changing too quickly -- last El Nino everyone was scratching their heads as to why the conditions ( for south east Australia ) were n't dry . " <p> Dr Opdyke said that El Nino used to cause drought in eastern Australia because it created cold conditions in the tropical waters to the north , and that is n't happening anymore as the waters were warmer than they used to be . <p> " Whenever we had monster El Ninos , the tropical waters became chilly first , " he said . <p> Dr Opdyke said climate scientists should not look to recent modelling for an idea of what would happen , but back more than 10,000 years ago to the last time conditions were like this . <p> " We have an analogous situation in the early Holocene when the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ pretty wet in the south east , " he said . <p> Parts of Australia can expect severe temperatures . <p> " I think we could see it get wetter rather than drier . " <p> CSIRO principal research scientist Wenju Cai , one of the climate scientists behind the paper in the Nature Climate Change journal , agreed that extreme wet weather was on the way , but not for the same reasons as Dr Opdyke . <p> He told the ABC the forecast El Nino would bring the hot dry conditions expected , but will be quickly followed by a La Nina in 2016 , which traditionally means extreme wet weather . <p> " This kind of extreme El Nino event normally lasts until April , May , and then we will see the system switch to conditions that are developing towards a La Nina in , say , next June , July , August , " he said . <p> He said the study he worked on found powerful El Nino and La Nina events will happen more often . <p> " What really was not good news @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Nina events , most of it will occur in the year after an extreme El Nino event , " he said . <p> " So under climate change , according to climate models , we are going to see this higher frequency of switching from El Nino to La Nina , one extreme to the opposite extreme , from one year to the next . " <p> An El Nino that lasted over the summer of 1982-83 saw a prolonged period of dry conditions that culminated in the Ash Wednesday bushfires , and was subsequently followed by floods in 1984 during La Nina . <p> La Nina conditions in 2011 were widely held responsible for the devastating floods in Queensland . <p> harry <p> I really cant see the human race going extinct anytime soon , but i can see mother nature doing a culling program , probably in a 100 years humans will be put in their place . <p> Abel Adamski <p> The rate at which things are happening is extremely concerning , we are forcing it along faster than ever before in the geological recrd . @@3525416 <h> The REDD scheme : putting a price on forests in order to save them <p> It 's called REDD and it 's designed to save endangered forests by recognising them as an economic commodity -- but one that 's worth more alive than dead . This contentious UN scheme is little known outside environmental circles , but as Antony Funnell reports , its potential impact could be huge . <p> As we puttered down a river in Indonesian Kalimantan it was impossible to miss : a vast section of forest stripped clear -- a great brown scar of dirt and mud . There are few things more confronting than the impact of logging in a pristine forest . <p> More logging camps awaited us beyond the next bend , some already eroded , with gullies formed where the earth on the side of the hill had gradually bled into the river below . <p> The idea of REDD is to find ways for people in the rich world ... to pay money to people in poor countries ... to protect their forests as an alternative to cutting @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ author <p> That was 2003 , and all of the logging I saw on that particular visit to Indonesia was legal , agreed to by government authorities because the trees of Kalimantan were worth valuable export dollars . <p> When we think of the wholesale destruction of forest reserves in a country like Indonesia , it 's easy to imagine the process as predominantly clandestine , involving illegal operators with chainsaws and handguns and corrupt local authorities paid to look the other way . But an overwhelming percentage of the logging that occurs in the developing world and elsewhere is officially sanctioned . <p> For decades , environmental organisations have been trying to save the world 's forests by appealing to non-economic considerations , like the need for biodiversity or the welfare of forest animals . Those appeals have often fallen on deaf ears . But a little known United Nations ' scheme called REDD is attempting to do something very different . <p> The scheme recognises the fact that forests have considerable economic value as timber , wood chips or as areas that can be cleared for commercial agriculture . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an economic commodity in and of themselves in their living state . <p> In essence , REDD -- which stands for Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation -- seeks to put a greater economic value on keeping trees alive , rather than chopping them down . It does this by trying to link the preservation of forests to a much broader environmental issue . <p> ' This is part of a strategy for fighting climate change , ' says science journalist and author Fred Pearce , ' for keeping as much carbon as we can in nature and preventing it leaking into the atmosphere as carbon dioxide and adding to the problems of global warming being caused by our own emissions from burning fossil fuels . <p> ' The idea of REDD is to find ways for people in the rich world with their big carbon emissions from power stations and so on , to pay money to people in poor countries ... to protect their forests as an alternative to cutting industrial emissions . ' <p> As such , REDD is expected to be on the agenda when delegates @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the 2015 United Nations Climate Change Conference . <p> The World Bank 's Elly Baroudy has high expectations : ' I think it is important that REDD is addressed in any kind of climate agreement , ' she says . ' If you look at the problems related to climate , it 's about where emissions are coming from . Land use and the way we manage land is a huge contributor to this issue , so if land does n't get to be part of the climate agreement then we are missing a big part of what could potentially be a solution in addressing climate change problems . ' <p> Like almost everything the United Nations does , REDD is extremely complex . It involves not just a carbon-pricing mechanism , but also , according to Baroudy , a system of guidelines for better forest management . <p> ' It 's really looking at how you can manage land in the interests of people . You are doing it for today and you are doing it for the future , ' she says . ' So it 's not only @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ management . <p> ' What we are trying to do in these funds is make sure there is that thinking process that happens going forward in planning , and it 's not a few years , it is really thinking 10 and 20 years into the future . ' <p> REDD is n't just complex , it 's also controversial . One person who 's pessimistic about what it can effectively achieve is Chris Lang , who runs an environmental website called REDD Monitor . <p> Lang accepts the scheme is well intentioned , but he argues that it fails to take into account the reality of entrenched corruption in the developing world . <p> ' Potentially REDD could lead to very large amounts of money pouring into these countries , but if it 's pouring into extremely corrupt governments and pouring into the forest sector of extremely corrupt governments , and often the forest sector is the most corrupt sector of a government , there is massive opportunity for corruption . ' <p> Lang also has doubts that any carbon price will ever be attractive enough for commercial operators . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to attract bigger profits from logging , mining or agriculture . <p> Countries participating in the UN REDD program <p> Concerns have also been expressed by the Center for International Forestry Research , headquartered in Bogor , Indonesia . <p> For several years now CIFOR has been researching 23 REDD trial sites in Brazil , Peru , Cameroon , Tanzania , Indonesia and Vietnam . <p> ' What we are aiming to do in our research is to measure the performance of REDD on a number of criteria , ' says principal scientist William Sunderlin . ' On its carbon effectiveness , its cost efficiency , the equity of the distribution of benefits , and the extent to which it is fulfilling a range of co-benefits , including poverty reduction and protection of rights and biodiversity protection . ' <p> That research is ongoing , but in 2014 Dr Sunderlin released his initial findings . The report card was mixed . While he applauded the scheme 's ambition , he also expressed doubts about the economic assumptions underpinning the whole initiative . <p> ' Part of the problem is that REDD @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by donor governments , and then the private sector was supposed to take over on the assumption that a robust market for forest carbon credits would emerge . There is a market for forest carbon credits , but it 's fairly small and the public sector dominates the funding for REDD . ' <p> Even there , according to Sunderlin , public sector support has been found wanting . <p> ' Economists who calculate the amount of funding that would be required for REDD to function in the way originally intended talk about " opportunity cost " which is the estimated cost of foregone forest conversion , and those estimates run anywhere from US$5 billion all the way up to US$60 billion per year . <p> ' To date , the total amount of funding generated for REDD is in the neighbourhood of $10 billion , and the Green Climate Fund , which is aiming to be a global fund for climate mitigation and adaptation has mobilised that amount of funding to date , which is a good thing , but REDD is still far away from generating the amount of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ concern identified by CIFOR involves the issue of land tenure . <p> REDD has being trialled in tropical forests , the ownership of which has historically been greatly contested . Corrupt governments in Asia , Africa and South America have often run roughshod over the wishes of poor local inhabitants ; ignoring or overriding the rights of forest-based communities in favour of commercial interests , in some cases even seizing people 's traditional land in order to on-sell it to foreign companies . <p> ' Although REDD proponents have been trying earnestly and in some cases effectively to lay an effective tenure foundation for REDD , ' says Sunderlin , ' in most cases they have encountered formidable challenges . ' <p> Worse still , according to Sunderlin , REDD initiatives have the potential to inadvertently hasten the disempowerment of local forest dwellers by increasing the economic value of forest land . It 's a concern shared by the Washington-based Rights and Resources Initiative , an international advocacy group that supports indigenous peoples . <p> ' The critical challenge that we see is implementation on the ground , ' says Jenny @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' Generally we feel that the most effective way for indigenous peoples and local communities to not be at risk is that their rights be legally recognised and implemented and supported by states . ' <p> However , when those states are corrupt , and are being offered large amounts of money to convert forest land into farmland , the task of ensuring indigenous tenure rights becomes difficult indeed . Springer acknowledges that REDD proponents are aware of the problem . <p> ' There have been a number of efforts , for example in the context of international discussions , to put in place safeguards . So there are safeguards that have been adopted for REDD specifically , ' she says . <p> Protecting the rights of indigenous communities and forest dwellers is crucial , says Springer , because they currently have legal tenure over around 15.5 per cent of the world 's 6.6 billion hectares of the forest and a proven track record of environmental protection . <p> ' Looking specifically at the role of communities in managing forests , there is a large amount of evidence that shows that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ support to manage them effectively , the environmental outcomes are very good . <p> ' Looking at the situation of the Brazilian Amazon , the largest and most important area of forests on the planet , the forest loss between 2000 and 2012 in the Brazilian Amazon was only 0.6 per cent inside legally recognised indigenous lands compared with 7 per cent outside indigenous lands . ' <p> While both the RRI and CIFOR remain cautious about the ultimate value of the REDD initiative , they both acknowledge the contribution it has made to the international debate so far . <p> William Sunderlin points to recent developments in Indonesia as a case in point : ' The historic decision two years ago by the constitutional court to grant large-scale rights to indigenous people to ownership of forests in Indonesia is really , when it comes right down to it , the product of the REDD debate globally and in Indonesia . <p> ' So there is concrete evidence of the ways in which the REDD experiment to date already has worked to assist indigenous tenure rights . This is not to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of indigenous people . That remains to be determined . But there is some evidence that it has been favourable to date , although with some setbacks . ' <p> All agree that REDD should , and is likely to , play a significant role during December 's Climate Change conference in Paris . ' Such an agreement would create the regulatory environment that would lay the foundation for robust funding of REDD , both public and private , ' says Sunderlin , but once again his enthusiasm is matched with scepticism . He describes the forecast for the December as ' ambiguous ' . <p> ' On the one hand , some historic problems around these negotiations have gone in a good direction . The differentiation between developed and non-developed countries -- that polarisation has ebbed . There 's a general sense now that all countries have to contribute . <p> ' But to date the phrasing of a possible agreement is weak , and that reflects the level of weak commitment and hesitation of countries to really step forward and make the kind of commitments that they need in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ can get behind . ' <p> For her part , the World Bank 's Elly Baroudy remains confident despite the uncertainty . <p> ' I 'm not a REDD negotiator and I 'm not directly involved in the negotiations , ' she says . ' But from where we sit we think that this is a very important issue that does need to be addressed within the climate negotiations . So far we see very positive signs . <p> ' There is a lot pinned on the hopes that Paris -- and REDD can be a piece of that -- is successful overall . '

@@3531186 <p> The cost to the economy of adopting a more ambitious emissions reduction target for 2030 is significantly less than claimed by Prime Minister Tony Abbott , the government 's own commissioned economic modelling reveals . <p> The Abbott government released two modelling reports late on Friday afternoon that helped underpin its choice of an emissions cut of 26 to 28 per cent cut from 2005 levels by 2030 as the target it will take to a major climate conference in Paris later this year . <p> In a joint press conference on August 11 to announce the targets , Mr Abbott quoted the modelling to say the target chosen by the government would result in lower gross domestic product of 0.2 and 0.3 per cent from what it would have otherwise would have been in 2030 . <p> But at the same press conference Mr Abbott went on to say : " The modelling that we have done suggests that achieving a 40 per cent reduction by then 2030 will be much more expensive . It will be over 2 per cent of GDP , so that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Advertisement <p> In fact the modelling found on the same figures that a cut of 45 per cent to emissions would result in a reduction in 2030 GDP of 0.5 to 0.7 per cent , significantly less than the Prime Minister claimed . <p> Asked about the discrepancy in the numbers , a spokesman for the Prime Minister said the figures quoted by Mr Abbott had instead come from " Labor 's own modelling " . He then referred Fairfax Media to Environment Minister Greg Hunt 's office , whose spokesman said it had come from 2013 economic modelling by the Climate Change Authority - an independent body set up by the former Gillard government to advise on climate policy . <p> Australia 's 2030 target has attracted criticism from many environmentalists because it falls behind the commitments of many other comparable nations , including the United States , Canada and New Zealand . <p> The two new modelling reports released were led by economist and former Reserve Bank board member Warwick McKibbin , and looked at the impact on the Australian economy of different domestic targets and the commitment to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ finds while GDP would be lower in 2030 than it otherwise would have been as a result of both domestic and international action , the Australian economy would continue to grow overall . <p> The yearly rate of GDP growth between 2020 to 2030 if Australia had no post-2020 target is modelled to be 2.21 per cent . If Australia adopted a 45 per cent cut to emissions , GDP growth over the same time period would by 2.09 per cent . <p> The modelling also found that Australian coal output would be 14 per cent lower in 2030 as a result of the emissions reduction target the Abbott government has announced . International pledges to cut emissions would add a further 8 per cent decline as countries move to adopt cleaner technology . <p> On the coal findings Professor McKibbin told Fairfax Media : " If you believe you can reduce CO2 emissions without changing the coal industry in the time frame we are talking about , that does n't appear to plausible . " But he also cautioned the numbers were highly uncertain . <p> Professor McKibbin said he @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about right " even if some claimed they was low by world standards , because the cost borne by Australia to reach the goal was higher compared to other countries . <p> Deputy chief executive of the Climate Institute Erwin Jackson said the modelling showed , as all other similar work had , that the economy could still grow strongly while emissions are cut . He said the modelling released on Friday did not include the economic benefits of acting on climate change , and more credible emissions reduction goals could have been announced if the use of international carbon permits had been part of the modelling mix . 
@@3533290 <h> How species respond to rising temperatures <p> It has already been happening for decades . Plants and animals are responding to rising temperatures . It is easier for animals , less so for plants . Species can sometimes change their physical location . They can also evolve as a response to changing conditions . Animals with short life spans and which breed profusely are ideal species to study these changes . Vanessa Kellerman and Carla Sgr ? at Monash University study how flies are responding to warmer conditions . Their ranges are changing , and their genetics is changing . Scientists are asking whether species are able to evolve fast enough for the rapidly changing climate and whether they are able to resist high heat . <h> Transcript <p> Robyn Williams : This is The Science Show on RN , where we also like to count flies . So we are interested in two evolutionary processes . There is changes in genes , and this is often considered to be a slower process . We are also interested in something else which is called plasticity , it 's just about being very flexible in your response , so it 's changes within a lifetime of an individual . Some people have made predictions that this response could buffer species from climate change . @@3540170 <h> The insights of Pope Francis in shaping Catholic health and aged care <p> These are hard times for the Catholic Church in Australia . Church attendance continues to decline . Mind you , I do think the encyclical would be all the stronger if it conceded that the growth in the world 's human population - from 2 billion when Pius XII first spoke of contraception to 3.5 billion when Paul VI promulgated Humanae Vitae to 7.3 billion and climbing as it is today - points to a need to reconsider the Church 's teaching on contraception . It would of course be ridiculous to suggest , as some of our critics do , that the Church 's teaching on birth control is a major factor contributing to climate change . The pope is quite right to insist that the reduction of population growth is not the only solution to the environmental crisis . But it is part of the solution . It may even be an essential part @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 7.3 billion people confronting the challenges of climate change and loss of biodiversity is a very different proposition from banning it in a world of only 2 billion people oblivious of such challenges . I doubt that you would find any papal adviser today who would advocate that the planet 's situation with climate change , loss of biodiversity , and water shortages would be improved if only all people of good will had declined to use artificial birth control for the last 50 years . I suspect the time is coming for Catholic health providers to lead the way distinguishing contraception from respect for all human life , especially at the bookends of the life cycle . By clinging to papal teaching on contraception , we put at risk the acceptance of the more fundamental teaching about the need to respect life -- both human life and the life of the planet . <p> Celebrating the 50th anniversary of Vatican II and preparing for the forthcoming Synod on the Family , we can take heart from the changes in our Church which permit and encourage such questions and dialogue . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is that it is no longer just liberal Catholics who are labeled as cafeteria Catholics . Some erstwhile conservative Catholics and papal apologists have become very exceptionalist in their discussion of this encyclical . We are now all welcome to the real world of questioning engagement in a Church that we cherish for its teaching office and sense of tradition . John O'Malley SJ , the finest contemporary historian of Vatican II writing in the English language has provided us with ' a simple litany ' of the changes in church style indicated by the council 's vocabulary : ' from commands to invitations , from laws to ideals , from threats to persuasion , from coercion to conscience , from monologue to conversation , from ruling to serving , from withdrawn to integrated , from vertical and top-down to horizontal , from exclusion to inclusion , from hostility to friendship , from static to changing , from passive acceptance to active engagement , from prescriptive to principled , from defiant to open-ended , from behaviour modification to conversion of heart , from the dictates of law to the dictates of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ holiness . ' <p> For you Catholic health providers , I will address three questions about Pope Francis 's encyclical Laudato Si ' which is ' on care for our common home ' . <p> Why would Pope Francis write to everyone ? <p> Pope Francis is not the first pope to address a social encyclical to everyone . Pope John Paul II addressed his 1988 encyclical Sollicitudo Rei Socialis to members of the Church and to ' all people of good will ' . Pope Benedict XVI did the same with his 2009 encyclical Caritas in Veritate . In comparison with his predecessors however , Francis has been more inclusive in the process of writing the encyclical and in the final content of the document . He quotes from 17 different conferences of Catholic bishops . This was rarely done by his predecessors . He is at pains to indicate that he is collaborative and that he takes the principle of subsidiarity very seriously . He convened meetings of various types of experts including scientists , economists and political scientists . He is not afraid to indicate that the final @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ authors . He notes , ' Although each chapter will have its own subject and specific approach , it will also take up and re-examine important questions previously dealt with .... Questions will not be dealt with once and for all , but reframed and enriched again and again . ' Being the final redactor of the text , he has felt free to interpolate some very folksy advice from time to time -- from the need to use less air conditioning , to the appropriateness of consumer boycotts on certain products , to the desirability of saying grace before and after meals . He has also taken the liberty of inserting some very blunt , evocative images of environmental and economic devastation : ' The earth , our home , is beginning to look more and more like an immense pile of filth . In many parts of the planet , the elderly lament that once beautiful landscapes are now covered with rubbish . ' He gives pride of place to Patriarch Bartholomew of Constantinople , the leader of 300 million Orthodox Christians . For the first time in a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the paleontologist Pierre Teilhard de Chardin -- although he could not quite bring himself to quoting him . He does quote the Protestant Paul Ricoeur who wrote " I express myself in expressing the world ; in my effort to decipher the sacredness of the world , I explore my own ' . Encyclicals characteristically end with a prayer composed by the Pope . We are given two prayers : one for Christians and one for all believers . This is a pope wanting to reach out to all persons who have a care for the environment and for the poor , regardless of their religious affiliations . <p> His concerns are not narrowly dogmatic or pedagogical but universally pastoral . He knows that millions of people , including erstwhile Catholics who work for Catholic health providers , are now suspicious of or not helped by notions of tradition , authority , ritual and community when it comes to their own spiritual growth which is now more individual and eclectic . He wants to step beyond the Church 's perceived lack of authenticity and its moral focus on individual matters , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ world is in a mess particularly with the state of the planet -- climate change , loss of biodiversity and water shortages , and with the oppression of the poor whose life basics are not assured by the operation of the free market , and with the clutter and violence of lives which are cheated the opportunity for interior peace . At the conclusion of the encyclical he describes the document as a ' lengthy reflection which has been both joyful and troubling ' . He is going to great pains to demystify his office and to demystify papal documents . Clearly he wants all people of good will to emulate him and to be both joyful and troubled as they wrestle with the problems of the age . <p> Why would Pope Francis have something to say about climate change ? <p> Francis thinks the planet risks going to hell in a basket . He says he is ' pointing to the cracks in the planet ' . Perhaps we should take heart from Leonard Cohen 's observation , ' There is a crack in everything . That 's how @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ home we have got . And the science is in . It indicates that climate change is real . The loss of biodiversity is real . Human activity continues to contribute adversely to both changes , though of course there are other causes . We can not undo the other causes . We do have the power to change and to address some of the human causes . An untrammeled free market will not provide the solution , neither will untrammeled governments whether they be self-seeking and corrupt or populist and short sighted . Francis sees an urgent need for people to be well educated , to be concerned about future generations , and to be focused beyond their national borders . He sees an urgent need for governments to abide by the rule of law . He sees an urgent need for markets to be regulated so that self-interest and economic imperatives can be better aligned to pay dividends for the planet and for future generations . He does n't see how this can be done unless more people , especially those designing laws and regulations for government and economic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ deep interior life marked by concern for neighbor and for creation as well as self . Francis calls us to consider the tragic effects of environmental degradation especially on the lives of the world 's poorest . <p> Hailing from Argentina , Francis puts his trust neither in ideological Communism nor in unbridled capitalism . Like his predecessors Benedict and John Paul II he is unapologetic asserting , ' By itself the market can not guarantee integral human development and social inclusion . ' His concern is not to settle arguments about politics , economics or science . He makes no pretence to give the last word on anything . He is not even much concerned to give the last word on scriptural interpretation or theological insights into topics such as anthropocentrism . He is wanting to enliven the passion and the spiritual commitment of his readers who , grasping the link between care for the earth , care for the poor , and care for the personal interior life , will be motivated to work for real change . <p> What new ideas are to be found in Pope Francis @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an honest and open debate , respecting the competencies of all , and inspired by the vision of St Francis of Assisi who is the model of the inseparable bond ' between concern for nature , justice for the poor , commitment to society , and interior peace ' . <p> There are probably no genuinely new ideas in the encyclical . Like many , he is convinced that we need to phase out our reliance on fossil fuels - coal , oil , ' and to a lesser degree , gas ' - progressively and without delay . He thinks any scheme for buying and selling carbon credits is deeply flawed . He is a great advocate for solar energy . But what is new is the integration of the scientific , the political , the sociological , the spiritual and the theological -- an integration given the stamp of approval of the leader of one of the world 's most significant religious communities . Granted that the Judeo-Christian tradition has done much to inculcate the notion that we humans are to subdue the earth , it is heartening that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ best way to restore men and women to their rightful place , putting an end to their claim to absolute dominion over the earth , is to speak once more of the figure of a Father who creates and who alone owns the world . Otherwise , human beings will always try to impose their own laws and interests on reality . <p> It could be even more helpful for us to move beyond the patriarchal view of God . It is not only the Church that has been complicit , but it has been complicit especially in ventures of colonisation aimed at plundering the resources of indigenous peoples . Francis notes , ' Modernity has been marked by an excessive anthropocentrism . ' He draws a good simple lesson from the Old Testament creation accounts noting : <p> The sheer novelty involved in the emergence of a personal being within a material universe presupposes a direct action of God and a particular call to life and to relationship on the part of a ' Thou ' who addresses himself to another ' thou ' . The biblical accounts of creation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ who can never be reduced to the status of an object . <p> Those of you who are not religious might garner the same sense by recalling Gemmy in the opening of David Malouf 's Remembering Babylon when he calls out , ' Do not shoot . I am a B-b-british object ! ' <p> In his folksy style , Francis notes that ' sobriety and humility were not favourably regarded in the last century ' . He calls us back to a ' serene attentiveness ' , reminding us in a grandfatherly way ' that being good and decent are worth it ' . Following the lead of the Australian bishops , he calls us to an ' ecological conversion ' , having a go at those ' committed and prayerful Christians ( who ) , with the excuse of realism and pragmatism , tend to ridicule expressions of concern for the environment . ' <p> Joy filled and troubled , let 's do something to change the market settings and political settings here in Australia to modify the behaviour of all Australians in the future , and let 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ care for the vulnerable and ' an integral ecology lived out joyfully and authentically ' . For starters , I should probably start rejoicing each time I catch the Murrays coach to Sydney rather than the Qantas jet , regardless of who 's paying . Even in the Canberra winter , I should also take to heart the Pope 's observation , ' A person who could afford to spend and consume more but regularly uses less heating and wears warmer clothes , shows the kind of convictions and attitudes which help to protect the environment . ' I wonder what any of this could mean to Catholic health providers running large carbon emitting operations . If the Australian government has been too modest with its targets , is there any way that you could further modify your admissions in line with what an appropriate national target would be ? Or would that simply be economic suicide for you ? Is there any place for a renowned , green Catholic health provider ? <p> When addressing Italian doctors last November , Pope Francis quoted St. Camillus de Lellis who suggested that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ simply to ' Put more heart into those hands . ' Francis said to the doctors : ' Put more heart in these hands ! This is also my hope . @@3540178 <h> Dry River wines of Martinborough a well-kept secret for too long <h> The New Zealand winery that 's becoming more inclusive of its fan base . <p> Dry River 's winemaker Wilco Lam is building on the foundations of his reclusive predecessors . Richard Brimer <p> by Philip Rich <p> Neil McCallum is a pioneer of New Zealand pinot and ? the Wairarapa wine region , otherwise known as Martinborough . A former research scientist with a PhD in organic chemistry from Oxford University , McCallum and wife Dawn planted the region 's first 100 vines in 1979 . They named their vineyard Dry River after a 19th century sheep station in the region , which itself was probably named due to the area 's dry , gravely and free-draining soil . <p> A couple of things spring to mind when I think of McCallum and Dry River Wines . The first -- and Dry River pinot fans wo n't like this -- is that despite its fame , I 've never particularly liked Dry River pinot . I 've always preferred Martinborough 's usually brilliant aromatic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ automatically associates with this part of the world . The second is that I rarely see Dry River wines . A relative recluse , McCallum -- who retired in 2011 -- eschewed wine shows and traditional marketing avenues such as trade events , dinners and public tastings in favour of selling primarily via his mailing list . He never even set up a cellar door . <p> I was , therefore , mildly surprised to receive an invitation to attend a lunch tasting in Melbourne to celebrate 30 vintages of Dry River . The host was to be Wilco Lam , the vineyard 's Dutch-born winemaker . Lam has been at Dry River since 2009 and chief winemaker since 2012 , when he took over from Poppy Hammond , who had been making the wine alongside McCallum since 2001 . <p> McCallum sold Dry River in 2003 to New York hedge fund billionaire Julian Robertson and Napa Valley winemaker Reg Oliver , the latter who died only two years later . Robertson , who co-founded Tiger Management LLC , at one time the world 's largest hedge fund , also owns @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ golf resorts along with Te Awa winery in Hawkes Bay . He fell in love with New Zealand on his first trip there in 1978 and now has residency as well as being an honorary knight , the latter bestowed in 2009 after he and wife Josie donated 15 late 19th to mid-20th century paintings by the likes of Picasso and Matisse to the Auckland Art Gallery , a gift estimated to be worth between NZ$120 million and NZ$150 million ( $134,000 ) . <p> McCallum and Robertson turned out to be a good match . Dry River was able to expand its vineyards , allowing McCallum to focus on both the viticulture and the resulting wine . It also became more inclusive , making greater attempts to connect with a new generation of wine drinkers , particularly since McCallum 's retirement . <p> What becomes clear over the course of the lunch -- which included wines made before Lam arrived as well as those he 's been completely responsible for -- is that Robertson has given Lam the freedom to make subtle changes without deviating greatly from Dry River 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ laid by McCallum and Hammond , in particular keeping the vineyard as the focus . But he is also changing things . " If it was up to Neil , we would n't be sitting here , " he tells me . " I want to share , have feedback and have an open conversation . " <h> Obsessed with viticulture <p> McCallum 's obsession with viticulture and , in particular , achieving optimal ripeness through a rigorous vineyard management program , has always set Dry River apart . That program involves leaf plucking , no irrigation and most interestingly , the laying down of white reflective mulch to refract light and thicken the skins in order to make the wines more concentrated and structured . <h> Related Quotes <h> Company Profile <p> This has worked to create textured , age-worthy aromatic whites that have power yet retain freshness , and a syrah that is both concentrated and beautifully aromatic . With the pinot it 's been a different story . The wine has always been quite extracted , lacking the nuance and subtlety of wines from Ata Rangi , Martinborough @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's 2012 and 2013 pinots , however , were a revelation . Lam partially removed the reflective mulch , believing that climate change and vine age were helping to get the grapes ripe enough . He has also reduced the hang time by picking slightly earlier . In the winery Lam has also made some subtle changes , pumping down the cap during fermentation two to three times a day , as opposed to the previous four to six times . <p> From a cool year , the 2012 has gorgeous aromatics and fine tannins , while the 2013 , which still retains some of Dry River 's trademark power and concentration , is beautifully balanced and far more nuanced than its predecessors . It 's a great set of new releases , demonstrating the deftness with which Lam is adding his own touch while remaining true to McCallum 's vision . <p> Philip Rich is a wine critic and judge , and co-owner of Prince Wine Store . <h> Reviews <p> Dry River Craighall Riesling 2014 , $75 <p> The superb 2009 riesling , bottled under cork as Dry River @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's riesling ages . The 2014 , which was picked early to retain acidity , has weight , texture and structure . Good now , it will only get better over the next five to eight years . <p> Dry River Pinot Noir 2013 , $140 <p> With 30 per cent whole bunches in the ferment and a gentler pigeage or pumping over than in the past , this has all the power that one associates with Dry River pinot but without any heaviness or over extraction . The fruit is pure and the wine is beautifully balanced . One of the best Dry River pinots I 've tasted . <p> Dry River Lovat Syrah 2011 , $95 <p> Its syrah has always been the best red in the Dry River range and the 2011 is right up there with anything coming out of Hawke 's Bay . Completely destemmed and matured in 300 litre hogsheads , only 20 per cent of which are new , this darkly fruited , gently spicy and vibrant wine is a joy to drink now but will continue to improve for some time .

The land where the cattle graze is leased from the Forest Service by third-generation rancher Steve Pargin . Several times a year , he and a crew led by his head cowboy , David Thompson , spend a week or more herding cattle from mountain range to mountain range to prevent them from causing damage to fragile ecosystems by staying in a single area too long . Picture taken June 11 , 2014 . Reuters/Lucas Jackson <p> Washington-based Center for Global Development has predicted that an India-sized piece of tropical forest could soon be gone if the current trend of deforestation continues . Using the data from satellite imagery , the researchers have further concluded that the complete elimination of 289 million hectares of tropical forest can take place by 2050 . <p> Based on the data and satellite imagery collected from over 100 countries , the researchers figured out @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ under threat of deforestation . The study , conducted by the team from the Center for Global Development , further suggests that the accelerating climate change could potentially result in some serious impact to the tropical forests . <p> The researchers have estimated that if the deforestation continues at the same pace , another 169 billion tonnes of carbon dioxide could be added to the atmosphere by 2050 . The estimated amount of carbon dioxide is equivalent to the gas released by 44,000 coal-powered plants , if ran continuously for over a year . <p> The environmental economist and study researcher , Jonah Busch , believes that one of the cheapest ways to fight climate change is to reduce the tropical deforestation . Busch further suggests putting a tax on carbon emissions to ensure that the countries around the globe are making an effort to protect their indigenous forests . <p> " The biggest driver of tropical deforestation by far is industrial agriculture to produce globally traded commodities , including soy and palm oil , " said Busc in a statement . The study predicts that if no efforts are taken @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the rate of deforestation is bound to increase between 2020 and 2030 , and expected to accelerate at around 2040 . <p> The Guardian reports that the study also cites examples of cases where effective control measures have been taken to manage deforestation , without impacting the total economic growth within the country . @@3543016 <h> Why the media 's war on science flies in the face of modern progress <h> Robyn Williams <p> Another triumph : Curiosity posts a selfie from the surface of Mars . Photo : NASA <p> Science works . You and I can tell . The intrepid space vehicle Curiosity lands on Mars after those seven minutes of terror and promptly goes roaming for samples . Rosetta , another long distance space shot , does some cosmic loops around planets , pulls up beside a comet and then lands its own little explorer -- which sent back evidence of rare organic molecules in the ice . New Horizons flew by Pluto last month after nine years and five billion kilometres and took immaculate pictures for us . What triumphs ! <p> Science works . Come up with bizarre maths and even weirder ? theories called quantum mechanics and apply them -- and you get the modern electronics industry . Every device in your pocket comes from this strange physics . Analyse the immune systems of young people , see how viral infections lead to cervical cancers and make @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ saved . <p> With all this evidence abounding and triumphs being displayed , how come science is being systematically trashed by propaganda machines ? How come that restrained even conservative magazine National Geographic in March had a cover story called The War on Science showing warning that climate research , evolutionary theory , vaccination and much else was being " discredited " ? How come the President of the Royal Society of London , Nobel Laureate Sir Paul Nurse , felt the need to front a BBC TV investigation again called The War on Science ? <p> In a world with fragmented information systems where many do n't bother with the traditional sources of news offered via TV , newspapers and radio , with their wide range of topics , you can instead seek out your own supply and end up with repeated confirmation of your own prejudices . The screen you look at shows little that 's unpredictable : it has become a mirror . <p> Advertisement <p> Meanwhile , the poor scientist is stuck behind the technicalities of the evidence at hand and needs to explain what those graphs @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that , and how he or she is there to discuss facts , not policy . And once you descend into nitpicking about the meaning of percentages or amounts of gases or fluctuations of numbers , the game is lost , the public is confused and the minister has gone off and done the deed anyway . <p> Several things flow from this manufactured confusion . The first is to recognise that we have a number of real challenges to face revealed by mountains of scientific evidence from impeccable sources . The second is that we must learn to seek out informed advice from those both expert in and trusted to interpret this evidence -- our academies ( Google them ) , our science magazines and you will quickly get effective summaries of what 's significant . <p> Then we need to know when we are being bullied . The War on Science has been going for about 10 years in its latest steroid state , it is well funded and relentless . The number of ads and websites spreading the message is undiminished . <p> So do we just sit @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ course not . Science has its own rigorous system of self-criticism and every young blood 's career is half set on finding fault with established ideas . Much of the fun of science is enjoying the arguments on a tricky topic . <p> Nor do we allow the worry about some strange creature , skink or snake , emitted gas or potential fouling of waterways to halt progress or wealth creation . Scientists deal with evidence . Policy is up to the rest of us . <p> The War on Science has stymied President Obama since he first walked in to the White House . He is now trying to use executive power to get minimal progress done . It could be that many problems we face have time limits on them and action is becoming urgent . When we have to deal with similar challenges involving our own health we have no choice but to act . Needless delay would be stupid , even scandalous . <p> The same applies to the wider world . <p> Robyn Williams is the presenter of the Science Show on ABC Radio and has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ so far <p> Great article Robyn , it seems to me the war on science seems to me to echo the rise of religious beliefs . @@3543152 <h> Setting aside half the Earth for ' rewilding ' : the ethical dimension <h> Author <h> Disclosure statement <p> William Lynn does not work for , consult , own shares in or receive funding from any company or organization that would benefit from this article , and has disclosed no relevant affiliations beyond the academic appointment above . <h> Translations <p> A much-anticipated book in conservation and natural science circles is EO Wilson 's Half-Earth : Our Planet 's Fight for Life , which is due early next year . It builds on his proposal to set aside half the Earth for the preservation of biodiversity . <p> The famous biologist and naturalist would do this by establishing huge biodiversity parks to protect , restore and connect habitats at a continental scale . Local people would be integrated into these parks as environmental educators , managers and rangers -- a model drawn from existing large-scale conservation projects such as Area de Conservaci ? n Guanacaste ( ACG ) in northwestern Costa Rica . <p> The backdrop for this discussion is that we are in the sixth great @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ being lost today than at any time since the end of the dinosaurs . There is no mystery as to why this is happening : it is a direct result of human depredations , habitat destruction , overpopulation , resource depletion , urban sprawl and climate change . <p> Wilson is one of the world 's premier natural scientists -- an expert on ants , the father of island biogeography , apostle of the notion that humans share a bond with other species ( biophilia ) and a herald about the danger posed by extinction . On these and other matters he is also an eloquent writer , having written numerous books on biodiversity , science , and society . So when Wilson started to talk about half-Earth several years ago , people started to listen . <p> As a scholar of ethics and public policy with an interest in animals and the environment , I have been following the discussion of half-Earth for some time . I like the idea and think it is feasible . Yet it suffers from a major blind spot : a human-centric view on the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and his seeming evolution on matters of ethics , is an invitation to explore how people ought to live with each other , other animals and the natural world , particularly if vast tracts are set aside for wildlife . <h> The ethics of Wilson 's volte-face <p> I heard Wilson speak for the first time in Washington , DC in the early 2000s . At that talk , Wilson was resigned to the inevitable loss of much of the world 's biodiversity . So he advocated a global biodiversity survey that would sample and store the world 's biotic heritage . In this way , we might still benefit from biodiversity 's genetic information in terms of biomedical research , and perhaps , someday , revive an extinct species or two . <p> Not a bad idea in and of itself . Still , it was a drearily fatalistic speech , and one entirely devoid of any sense of moral responsibility to the world of nonhuman animals and nature . <p> What is striking about Wilson 's argument for half-Earth is not the apparent about-face from cataloging biodiversity to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to it . In several interviews , he references the need for humanity to develop an ethic that cares about planetary life , and does not place the wants and needs of a single species ( Homo sapiens sapiens ) above the well-being of all other species . <p> To my ear , this sounds great , but I am not exactly sure how far it goes . In the past , Wilson 's discussions of conservation ethics appear to me clearly anthropocentric . They espouse the notion that we are exceptional creatures at the apex of evolution , the sole species that has intrinsic value in and of ourselves , and thus we are to be privileged above all other species . <p> In this view , we care about nature and biodiversity only because we care about ourselves . Nature is useful for us in the sense of resources and ecological services , but it has no value in and of itself . In ethics talk , people have intrinsic value while nature 's only value is what it can do for people -- extrinsic value . <p> For @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Wilson argues for " the necessity of a robust and richly textured anthropocentric ethics apart from the issues of rights for other animals or ecosystems -- one based on the hereditary needs of our own species . In addition to the well-documented utilitarian potential of wild species , the diversity of life has immense aesthetic and spiritual value . " <p> The passage indicates Wilson 's long-held view that biodiversity is important because of what it does for humanity , including the resources , beauty and spirituality people find in nature . It sidesteps questions of whether animals and the rest of nature have intrinsic value apart from human use . <p> His evolving position , as reflected in the half-Earth proposal , seems much more in tune with what ethicist call non-anthropocentrism -- that humanity is simply one marvelous but no more special outcome of evolution ; that other beings , species and/or ecosystems also have intrinsic value ; and that there is no reason to automatically privilege us over the rest of life . <p> What kind of a species are we that we treat the rest of life @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the destiny of Earth : we arrived , we 're humanizing the Earth , and it will be the destiny of Earth for us to wipe humans out and most of the rest of biodiversity . But I think the great majority of thoughtful people consider that a morally wrong position to take , and a very dangerous one . <p> The non-anthropocentric view does not deny that biodiversity and nature provide material , aesthetic and spiritual " resources . " Rather , it holds there is something more -- that the community of life has value independent of the resources it provides humanity . Non-anthropocentric ethics requires , therefore , a more caring approach to people 's impact on the planet . Whether Wilson is really leaving anthropocentrism behind , time will tell . But for my part , I at least welcome his opening up possibilities to discuss less prejudicial views of animals and the rest of nature . <p> These organizations use a mix of conservation science , education and public policy initiatives to promote protecting and restoring continental-scale habitats and corridors , all with an eye to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ One example is ongoing work to connect the Yellowstone to Yukon ecosystems along the spine of the Rocky Mountains . <p> When I was a graduate student , the term half-Earth had not yet been used , but the idea was in the air . My classmates and I referred to it as the " 50% solution . " We chose this term because of the work of Reed Noss and Allen Cooperrider 's 1994 book , Savings Nature 's Legacy . Amongst other things , the book documents that , depending on the species and ecosystems in question , approximately 30% to 70% of the original habitats of the Earth would be necessary to sustain our planet 's biodiversity . So splitting the difference , we discussed the 50% solution to describe this need . <p> This leads directly into my third point . The engagement of Wilson and others with the idea of half-Earth and rewilding presupposes but does not fully articulate the need for an urban vision , one where cities are ecological , sustainable and resilient . Indeed , Wilson has yet to spell out what we @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to maintaining and teaching about his proposed biodiversity parks . This is not a criticism , but an urgent question for ongoing and creative thinking . <p> Humans are urbanizing like never before . Today , the majority of people live in cities , and by the end of the 21st century , over 90% of people will live in a metropolitan area . If we are to meet the compelling needs of human beings , we have to remake cities into sustainable and resilient " humanitats " that produce a good life . <p> Such a good life is not to be measured in simple gross domestic product or consumption , but rather in well-being -- freedom , true equality , housing , health , education , recreation , meaningful work , community , sustainable energy , urban farming , green infrastructure , open space in the form of parks and refuges , contact with companion and wild animals , and a culture that values and respects the natural world . <p> To do all this in the context of saving half the Earth for its own sake is a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are up to if we have the will and ethical vision to value and coexist in a more-than-human world . 
@@3543210 <h> How understanding the prisoner 's dilemma can help bridge liberal and conservative differences <h> Author <h> Disclosure statement <p> Dylan Selterman does not work for , consult , own shares in or receive funding from any company or organization that would benefit from this article , and has disclosed no relevant affiliations beyond the academic appointment above . <p> In my social psychology class , I pose an extra credit question where students choose between having two points or six points added onto their final term paper grade , with the stipulation that if more than 10% of the class chooses six points , no one gets any points . <p> This exercise is a classroom demonstration of the commons dilemma , and similar to the prisoner 's dilemma . Essentially , people are forced to choose between what would maximize their personal outcomes ( more points ) and what would be best for the group as a whole ( fewer points ) . <p> It 's worth noting that this exercise was developed 25 years ago . I first learned it from my college psychology professor @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ using it since 2008 . <p> But recently , after a student of mine tweeted the dilemma of the extra credit question , it went viral in a way that I had never expected . So why is it only now starting to resonate with so many people worldwide ? And why are people connecting this exercise to concerns about greed or selfishness ? <h> The prisoner 's dilemma <p> Let 's analyze this class exercise . At first glance , it would seem that the obvious choice would be to pick two points -- for then , everyone is sure to get the points . <p> But this requires a great deal of social trust . And that is not always apparent between strangers . <p> Thus , some students choose six points ( greater than 10% of students in all of my classes have done so , except one class which hit 10% exactly ) . In fact , I would argue , picking six is a " rational " choice , because the likelihood of your own choice directly affecting the group is very small . <p> Now @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ if everyone in a group uses this line of " rational " reasoning . Then everyone would proceed to behave in a way that maximizes their own lot . The point here is that choosing six is " rational , " but only when we consider how individual actions impact the group . <p> In the aggregate , when thousands ( or millions ) of people behave this way , the consequences are disastrous . <p> This exercise is analogous to real-world behavior involving consumption of public resources ( water , food , oil , electricity , etc ) . The " rational " mindset is how we end up with overharvesting , water or food shortages , pollution , climate change , etc . <h> What the exercise revealed <p> It 's important to note that most students in my class ( around 80% each semester ) end up choosing two points . While many students choose the " rational " six-point option , they are still in the minority . <p> I believe this is because most people do understand the importance of being communal . In other words @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that benefits others around them . <p> Why is it so ? Humans are prosocial creatures -- which means they like to help each other . <p> That most students chose the prosocial option in my class is notable . It inspires me and gives me hope for the future . However , the fight is not over , and we still need to reduce excessive consumption . <h> People crave reciprocity <p> So , learning from this exercise , how can we increase cooperation on a mass scale ? <p> People crave reciprocity with others . If someone does us a favor , we feel compelled to repay the kindness : or if they hurt us , we crave revenge . <p> Fairness manifests in justice and equality ( eg , right to a fair trial ) , and in principles like the " Golden Rule " ( treating people the same way you want them to treat you ) . <p> Another moral virtue is in-group loyalty . <p> Every community and nation has important symbols of unity ( eg , the national flag ) , songs , pledges @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sacred documents ( eg , the Constitution ) , and institutions designed for the group 's protection ( eg , the military ) . <p> In recent history , liberals have tended to strongly emphasize the importance of fairness and justice in building a strong society . <p> Consider the equal rights movements for African Americans , women , and LGBTQ folks , especially in light of the recent Supreme Court ruling . Liberals are fighting to close wage gaps , end discrimination , and promote equal rights . <p> In contrast , conservatives have tended to emphasize the importance of group loyalty . Consider the emphasis on keeping America strong , protecting the homeland from foreign threats , bolstering the military and respecting national symbols like the flag . <h> Challenges outside the classroom <p> So how do these moral virtues apply to the commons dilemma game ? <p> Well , if you want the extra points , you 're relying on other people to cooperate . So , think about the ethic of fairness . <p> Pick the same choice that you would want others to take . Let @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ personal decisions -- if you want others to choose two points , you should do the same . <p> Additionally , if you want your group ( eg , your school , your community ) to thrive , you must personally contribute . If you care about the health and the spirit of your culture , that sentiment must be reflected in your own actions . <p> If we consider the ethic of group loyalty , then choosing two points is not only cooperative , it 's patriotic . Making a conscious effort to limit one 's consumption of resources ( by using less water , for example ) is a duty to the flags , symbols and pledges of allegiance that unite us . <p> Outside of the classroom setting , there are environmental problems that must be solved , and there are moral virtues that can help bridge across the ideological aisle . <p> While liberals and conservatives may differ in their perspectives on political issues , there are ways to build common ground by drawing on their respective moral concerns . <h> Solving the world 's problems <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ selfishness and making cooperation more possible . <p> If we can harness the power of these moral virtues together , we just might have a shot at solving some of the world 's toughest ecological problems . <p> In order to combat climate change , we need to use all the tools available in our moral toolkit . Everyone must sacrifice for the common good of preserving our great nation , and it is essential that we view our neighbors as equal partners in this endeavor. 
@@3546076 <h> The ' green-tech ' future is a flawed vision of sustainability <h> Author <h> Disclosure statement <p> Samuel Alexander does not work for , consult , own shares in or receive funding from any company or organization that would benefit from this article , and has disclosed no relevant affiliations beyond the academic appointment above . <p> What does your vision of a sustainable future look like ? Some people imagine a scenario whereby technology solves the world 's most pressing environmental problems . <p> In this world we all drive electric cars and have solar panels on our roofs that power our air conditioners and flat-screen televisions . We purchase " eco " products that provide all the convenience and comfort but without degrading the planet . We continue consuming and growing our economies , yet Mother Nature wins too . <p> But I and my colleague Josh ( who co-wrote this article ) would argue that this vision of sustainability is flawed , and will in fact drive greater damage to the world , its ecosystems , and us . So how has this vision @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ so popular ? <p> There seem to be three main reasons why the " green-tech " conception of sustainability is dominant . <p> First , it is good for business . Sustainability is presented as something we can either purchase as consumers or sell as green entrepreneurs . There is no conflict here between consumer capitalism and sustainability , so the powers that be need not feel threatened . As the sustainable design website Inhabitat declares : " Design will save the world " . <p> Second , the green-tech future is politically palatable . Politicians can defend their " green " credentials without questioning the affluence that consumerist cultures seem to expect and demand . From this perspective , sustainability is a great opportunity for green growth of the economy , which means that politicians do not have argue that we need to consume less . <p> Third , the green-tech future is less challenging for those of us who enjoy the affluent lifestyles that make such tremendous demands on natural resources . God forbid anyone suggest that sustainability requires that we rethink our lifestyles ! As George Bush Snr @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ) : " the American way of life is not negotiable " . <p> Fortunately , for all those cultures that emulate the American way of life , the dominant faith in a green-tech future means that we can enjoy consumer affluence in good conscience , knowing that it is consistent with the transition to a just and sustainable world . We just need to do consumerism better , and given that human beings are so clever , that should n't be a problem . Right ? <h> Why the ' green-tech ' future is flawed <p> The fact that the green-tech vision does n't fundamentally challenge business , politics , or our lifestyles should perhaps give us cause to question its validity . When things sound a bit too good to be true , they are often false . <p> The best way to expose the myth of the green-tech future is to stare unflinchingly at the state of the world today , an undertaking designed not to invoke despair but to help us better understand what an appropriate response would look like . <p> The ecological footprint analysis indicates @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . According to the latest research , we would need 1.6 planets if the existing civilisation were to be sustained over the long term . <p> Will science save the day ? Despite decades of extraordinary technological and scientific advance , humanity 's economic activity continues to devastate the ecosystems on Earth . Over the past 40 years alone , 50% of wildlife on the planet has been destroyed . <p> Furthermore , the climate problem is far worse than most people think . Kevin Anderson , a climate scientist at the Tyndall Centre for Climate Change Research , has shown that stopping global temperatures from rising 2C above pre-industrial levels means that the richest nations need to decarbonise their economies by 8-10% per year over the coming decades . <p> Given the close connection between energy and economics , such swift decarbonisation is incompatible with conventional growth economics . <p> What makes this " limits to growth " analysis all the more challenging is that the global population , currently at 7.3 billion , continues to increase . The latest evidence suggests that we 're trending towards 11 billion by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's most successful ecovillages have yet to attain a " fair share " ecological footprint . <p> Do we need to go on ? The point is that globalising Western-style affluence to the world 's expanding population would be catastrophic . Technology and design improvements , on their own , simply can not make that development agenda sustainable . <p> It follows that if we are to achieve a world where the entire community of life can flourish within planetary limits , we need to begin with a new vision of sustainability . <h> A different version of the good life <p> Our argument is that genuine sustainability implies a different vision of the good life -- one arising out of the unfashionable virtues of moderation , frugality , and sufficiency . We can consider this in terms of aesthetics : what we find appealing or attractive . <p> We must come to see the wind turbines that grace our landscapes as one of the best aspects of civilisation , something to admire , and not as " utterly offensive " blights , as our political leaders suggest . But even @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reducing demand , which also changes the vision of what sustainability looks like . <p> Rather than instinctively turning on the heater , let us first ensure we are wearing a warm woollen sweater . If the heater is turned on , heat only one room , just to take the chill off the air . Let us dress creatively in second-hand or mended clothing , or even make our own , rather than be seduced onto the consumerist treadmill by the marketing and fashion industries . <p> While electric cars may have a limited role to play on the path to a sustainable world , the more important transition will be the increase of cycling , walking , and public transport . A local camping trip , where we reconnect with nature under the stars , represents a lost luxury that can easily replace a trip to Bali . <p> Let us dig up our Victorian lawns and grow organic food and keep a few chickens for eggs . Even our dinner plates will look different : advocating a low-meat or no-meat diet is the cultural taboo the environmental movement @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of this implies going back to the stone age . It 's about focusing on what is sufficient to live well . <h> Will wearing a woollen sweater stop capitalism ? <p> No , but that 's not really the point . We 're not attempting to present a new political or macro-economic model here ( although some ideas have been sketched elsewhere ) . But we are arguing that we need to think of sustainability in a different way . <p> Our question is not : how can we make the existing way of life sustainable ? Our question is : what would a sustainable way of life look like ? And part of the answer involves embracing a simpler way . <p> By reusing , reducing , reclaiming , and recycling so much of our current waste streams , our gardens , homes and suburbs may come to look like a creative DIY zone -- not fancy , but functional . This is not the eco-future that we are shown in glossy design magazines featuring million-dollar " green homes " that are prohibitively expensive . <p> Ours is a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- conception of sustainability . We hold up the water tank over the desalination plant . <p> Do not mistake us , however . While we do reject the Jetsons as a vision of a sustainable future , our purpose is not to celebrate the Flintstones . Obviously we need to advance science and embrace appropriate technologies , especially those that produce renewable energy . <p> But we do call for a more humble aesthetics of sustainability , one that sees ugliness in the clothes dryer and elegance in the clothesline . <p> This article was co-authored by Josh Floyd , advisor on energy , systems , and societal futures at the Understandascope , and founding partner of the Centre for Australian Foresight .
